\{\mu_{11,\ell},\sigma_{1,\ell}^{2}\}_{\ell=1}^{\infty}
satisfies
(\mu_{11,\ell},\sigma_{1,\ell}^{2})\rightarrow(Y_{11},0)
as
\ell\rightarrow\infty
such that

\frac{\mu_{11,\ell}-Y_{11}}{\sigma_{1,\ell}}\rightarrow C\quad\text{as $\ell\rightarrow\infty$,}

where
C
is some constant. Then, for any positive and large constant
B>0
, we have
LR_{n}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{2,\ell})>B
by taking a sufficiently large value of
\ell
because

\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\sigma_{1,\ell}}\phi\left(\frac{\mu_{11,\ell}-Y_{11}}{\sigma_{1,\ell}}\right)=\phi\left(C\right)\times\lim_{\sigma_{1,\ell}\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{\sigma_{1,\ell}}=\infty.

The analogous argument applies to the model (
8
) with normal mixture density (
10
), and the stated result of part (ii) follows.

∎

Proof of Proposition
2
.

Note that, for
Z=\tfrac{Y-\mu}{\sigma}
,

\frac{\partial}{(\partial\mu)}\Bigl[\tfrac{1}{\sigma}\,\phi(Z)\Bigr]\;=\;\tfrac{Z}{\sigma}\;\frac{1}{\sigma}\,\phi(Z),\quad\frac{\partial^{2}}{(\partial\mu)^{2}}\Bigl[\tfrac{1}{\sigma}\,\phi(Z)\Bigr]\;=\;\tfrac{Z^{2}-1}{\sigma^{2}}\;\frac{1}{\sigma}\,\phi(Z),

\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma^{2}}\Bigl[\tfrac{1}{\sigma}\,\phi(Z)\Bigr]\;=\;\tfrac{1}{2\,\sigma^{2}}\,(Z^{2}-1)\;\frac{1}{\sigma}\,\phi(Z).

Hence, for either
j=1,2
, a straightforward calculation gives

\left.\frac{\partial^{2}g_{2}}{(\partial\mu_{j})^{2}}\right|_{(\bar{\alpha}.\theta^{*},\theta^{*})}\;=\bar{\alpha}\;f^{*}\,\frac{1}{\sigma^{*2}}\,\Bigl[\,(Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{T})^{2}-T\Bigr]\quad\text{and}\quad\left.\frac{\partial\,g_{2}}{\partial\sigma_{j}^{2}}\right|_{(\bar{\alpha}.\theta^{*},\theta^{*})}\;=\bar{\alpha}\;f^{*}\,\frac{1}{2\,\sigma^{*2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bigl(Z_{t}^{2}-1\bigr),

(58)

where

Z_{t}:=\frac{Y_{t}-\mu^{*}}{\sigma^{*}},\qquad t=1,\dots,T.

Suppose, for contradiction, that real constants
a,b
exist such that

\left.\frac{\partial^{2}g_{2}}{(\partial\mu_{j})^{2}}\right|_{(\bar{\alpha}.\theta^{*},\theta^{*})}\;=\;a\;+\;b\,\left.\frac{\partial\,g_{2}}{\partial\sigma_{j}^{2}}\right|_{(\bar{\alpha}.\theta^{*},\theta^{*})}

with positive probability. Since
\bar{\alpha}f^{*}/\sigma^{*2}>0
almost surely, substituting (
58
) into the above equation, and dividing both sides by
\bar{\alpha}f^{*}/\sigma^{*2}
gives

(Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{T})^{2}\;-\;T\;=\;\frac{a\,\sigma^{*2}}{\bar{\alpha}f^{*}}\;+\;\frac{b}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bigl(Z_{t}^{2}-1\bigr),

or equivalently

(Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{T})^{2}\;=\;\frac{b}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}^{2}\;+\;\Bigl(\frac{a\,\sigma^{*2}}{\bar{\alpha}f^{*}}-\tfrac{b\,T}{2}\Bigr)\;+\;T.

But

(Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{T})^{2}\;=\;\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}^{2}\;+\;2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq T}Z_{i}Z_{j},

so this would force

\Bigl(1-\tfrac{b}{2}\Bigr)\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}^{2}\;+\;2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq T}Z_{i}Z_{j}\;=\;C,\quad C\;:=\;\frac{a\,\sigma^{*2}}{\bar{\alpha}f^{*}}\;-\;\tfrac{b\,T}{2}\;+\;T,

as a polynomial identity in
(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{T})
. Since the coefficient of each cross‐term
Z_{i}Z_{j}
on the left is
2
, but on the right must be
0
, no choice of
b
can satisfy this identity except on a set of probability zero. Hence no such constants
a,b
exist, and

\Pr\Bigl[\,\left.\tfrac{\partial^{2}g_{2}}{(\partial\mu_{j})^{2}}\right|_{(\bar{\alpha}.\theta^{*},\theta^{*})}\;=\;a\;+\;\left.b\,\tfrac{\partial\,g_{2}}{\partial\sigma_{j}^{2}}\right|_{(\bar{\alpha}.\theta^{*},\theta^{*})}\Bigr]\;=\;0.

∎

Proof of Proposition
3
.

Our proof closely follows the proof of Theorem 3.3 in
Hathaway, 1985
by verifying Assumptions 1, 2, 3, and 5 of
Kiefer and Wolfowitz, 1956
.

We first consider a model with the component-specific density function (
1
) with (
2
).
Because our model has additional free parameters
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}
s, as in the proof of
Kasahara and Shimotsu, 2015
,
we consider the joint density of
m_{q}:=M(q+1)
observations instead of
M+1
observations in
Hathaway, 1985
, where
q:=\text{dim}(\boldsymbol{\beta})
. The joint density function of
m_{q}
observations is itself a mixture of
M^{m_{q}}
components, where each component is given by
\prod_{j=1}^{m_{q}}\prod_{t=1}^{T}f(Y_{jt};\mu_{i_{j}}+\boldsymbol{X}_{jt}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i_{j}},\sigma_{i_{j}})
for some choices
i_{j}\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
, with the density of
N(\mu,\sigma^{2})
denoted by
f(y;\mu,\sigma):=\frac{1}{\sigma}\phi((y-\mu)/\sigma)
.

Assumptions 1, 2, and 3 of
Kiefer and Wolfowitz, 1956
are easily verified for the joint density of
m_{q}
observations. We verify Assumption 5 of
Kiefer and Wolfowitz, 1956
for the joint density function of
m_{q}
observations by showing that

E\left[\log\prod_{j=1}^{m_{q}}\prod_{t=1}^{T}f(Y_{jt};\mu_{i_{j}}^{*}+\boldsymbol{X}_{jt}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i_{j}}^{*},\sigma^{*}_{i_{j}})\right]>-\infty

(59)

for
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}^{*}_{M}\in\Theta_{M}^{*}
and that

E\sup_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}\in\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}}(\boldsymbol{c})}\left[\log\prod_{j=1}^{m_{q}}\prod_{t=1}^{T}f(Y_{jt};\mu_{i_{j}}+\boldsymbol{X}_{jt}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i_{j}},\sigma_{i_{j}})\right]<\infty

(60)

for all component choices
i_{j}\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
,
which correspond to equations (3.1) and (3.4) in
Hathaway, 1985
, respectively. (
59
) follows from the argument in the proof of Theorem 3.3 of
Hathaway, 1985
.

For (
60
), because
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}\in{\bar{\Theta}}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}}(\boldsymbol{c})
, there exists
c\in(0,1]
such that
\min_{j,k}\sigma_{j}/\sigma_{k}>c
.
Proceeding as in
Hathaway, 1985
, we can show that

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}\in\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}}(\boldsymbol{c})}\log\left[\prod_{j=1}^{m_{q}}\prod_{t=1}^{T}f(Y_{jt};\mu_{i_{j}}+\boldsymbol{X}_{jt}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i_{j}},\sigma_{i_{j}})\right]

is no larger than, for some
\ell\in\{1,...,M\}
and
j_{1},j_{2},...,j_{q+1}\in\{1,...,m_{k}\}
,

\sup_{\mu_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\ell},\sigma_{\ell}}\log\left[\delta(\sigma_{\ell})\prod_{r=1}^{q+1}\prod_{t=1}^{T}f(Y_{j_{r}t};\mu_{\ell}+\boldsymbol{X}_{j_{r}t}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\ell},\sigma_{\ell})\right],

(61)

where
\delta(\sigma_{\ell})=(2\pi)^{-T(M-1)(q+1)/2}(c\sigma_{\ell})^{-T(M-1)(q+1)}
, because
f(Y_{jt};\mu_{i_{j}}+\boldsymbol{X}_{jt}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i_{j}},\sigma_{i_{j}})=(2\pi)^{-1/2}(\sigma_{i_{j}})^{-1}\exp(-\{Y_{jt}-(\mu_{i_{j}}+\boldsymbol{X}_{jt}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i_{j}})\}^{2}/2\sigma_{i_{j}}^{2})\leq(2\pi)^{-1/2}(\sigma_{i_{j}})^{-1}\leq(2\pi)^{-1/2}(c\sigma_{\ell})^{-1}
for
j\notin\{j_{1},j_{2},...,j_{q+1}\}
.

Note that
\prod_{q=1}^{q+1}\prod_{t=1}^{T}f(Y_{j_{q}t};\mu_{\ell}+\boldsymbol{X}_{j_{q}t}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\ell},\sigma_{\ell})
is the likelihood function of a linear Gaussian model. Therefore, the maximized value of (
61
) equals
C_{1}-C_{2}\log(SSR)
,
where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are a finite constant that depends only on
M
,
k
, and
T
while
SSR
is the sum of squared residuals obtained from regressing
\{\{Y_{j_{q}t}\}_{t=1}^{T}\}_{q=1}^{q+1}
on
\{\{1,\boldsymbol{X}_{j_{q}t}\}_{t=1}^{T}\}_{q=1}^{q+1}
. Because we have
(T-1)(q+1)
more observations than the number of parameters, the SSR is distributed as
\sigma_{\ell}^{*2}\chi^{2}((T-1)(q+1))
. Since
E\log(\chi^{2}((T-1)(q+1)))<\infty
, the expected value of (
61
) is finite, and (
60
) holds. This verifies Assumption 5 of
Kiefer and Wolfowitz, 1956
, and the stated consistency result follows.

When the component-specific density function is given by (
1
) with (
3
), where the number of components in (
3
) is
K
, the joint density function of
m_{q}
observations can be written as a mixture of
M^{m_{q}}\times K^{m_{q}T}
components,
where each component is given by
\prod_{j=1}^{m_{q}}\prod_{t=1}^{T}f(Y_{jt};\mu_{i_{j}i_{k}}+\boldsymbol{X}_{jt}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i_{j}},\sigma_{i_{j}})
for some choices
i_{j}\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
and
i_{k}\in\{1,...,K\}
. Then, repeating the same argument as above, we can show that the expected value of

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}\in\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}}(\boldsymbol{c})}\log\left[\prod_{j=1}^{m_{q}}\prod_{t=1}^{T}f(Y_{jt};\mu_{i_{j}i_{k}}+\boldsymbol{X}_{jt}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i_{j}},\sigma_{i_{j}})\right]
is bounded, and Assumption 5 of
Kiefer and Wolfowitz, 1956
holds. Because Assumptions 1, 2, and 3 of
Kiefer and Wolfowitz, 1956
are also easily verified, the consistency result follows.

The proof for the dynamic model with the component-specific density function (
8
) using either (
9
) or (
10
) follows analogously and is therefore omitted.

∎

Proof of Proposition
4
.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition 3 in
Kasahara and Shimotsu, 2015
.

Given the value of
\boldsymbol{c}
and
\alpha\in[c_{1},1-c_{1}]
, define the space for reparameterized parameters as

\boldsymbol{\psi}:=(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\top},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\top})^{\top}\in\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\alpha}}
,
where
\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\alpha}}=\{\boldsymbol{\psi}=(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\top},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\top})^{\top}:(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\nu}+(1-\alpha)\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\nu}-\alpha\boldsymbol{\lambda})\in\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{c})\}.
Under the null hypothesis
H_{01}:\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
, we have
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\boldsymbol{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
. We rewrite the reparameterized parameters under the null hypothesis as
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}=((\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})^{\top},\boldsymbol{0}^{\top})^{\top}
. We denote the reparameterized density function and its logarithm as

\displaystyle g(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)

\displaystyle:=\alpha f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\nu}+(1-\alpha)\boldsymbol{\lambda})+(1-\alpha)f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\nu}-\alpha\boldsymbol{\lambda})\ \text{ and }\ l(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)=\log g(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha).

(62)

Let
L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}l(\boldsymbol{W}_{i};\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)
be the reparameterized log-likelihood function.
For each
\alpha\in[c_{1},1-c_{1}]
, define the reparameterized MLE as

\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\alpha}=\arg\max_{\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\alpha}}}L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha).

(63)

Collect the relevant normalized reparameterized parameters and define
\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)
as

\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\\
\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\alpha)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\nu}-\boldsymbol{\nu}^{*}\\
\alpha(1-\alpha)\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\end{pmatrix},

(64)

where
v(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
is given by (
26
).

As discussed in the proof of Lemma
4
, taking the fourth-order Taylor expansion of
L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)
around
(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)
, we may write
2\{L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)-L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)\}
as a quadratic function of
\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)
as

\displaystyle 2\{L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)-

\displaystyle L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)\}=2(\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha))^{\top}\boldsymbol{S}_{n}-(\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha))^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha))+R_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)

(65)

\displaystyle\quad=\boldsymbol{G}_{n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}\boldsymbol{G}_{n}-\left[\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)-\boldsymbol{G}_{n}\right]^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}\left[\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)-\boldsymbol{G}_{n}\right]+R_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha),

(66)

where
\boldsymbol{S}_{n}:=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{s}}(\boldsymbol{W}_{i})
and
\boldsymbol{G}_{n}:=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}^{-1}\boldsymbol{S}_{n}
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}
is the negative of the sample Hessian defined in the proof of Lemma
4
.

Noting that
L_{n}(\boldsymbol{{\psi}}^{*},\alpha)=L_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}_{0},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{0})
,
write

\displaystyle LR_{n}

\displaystyle=\max_{\alpha\in[c_{1},1-c_{2}]}2\{L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}_{\alpha},\alpha)-L_{n}(\boldsymbol{{\psi}}^{*},\alpha)\}-2\{L_{0,n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{0},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{0})-L_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}_{0},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{0})\}.

(67)

Define

\boldsymbol{S}_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}n}\\
\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{W}_{i})\\
n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{W}_{i})\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{matrix}\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}n}:=\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}n},\quad\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}n}:=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}n},\\
\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\lambda}}:=\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\alpha),\end{matrix}

and split the quadratic form in (
65
) to obtain

\displaystyle 2\{L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)-L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)\}=B_{n}(\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\lambda}})+C_{n}(\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\alpha))+R_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha),

(68)

where

\begin{split}B_{n}(\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\lambda}})&=2\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}n}-\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\lambda}},\\
C_{n}(\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})&=2\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}n}-\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\\
&=\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}n}-(\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}-\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}n})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}-\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}n}),\end{split}

(69)

with

\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}n}\overset{d}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{G}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}=(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}})^{-1}\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}
and
\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}n}\overset{d}{\to}\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}\sim N(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}})
.
In addition,
R_{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\alpha},\alpha)=o_{p}(1)
holds from Lemma
4
(a) and
\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\alpha},\alpha)=O_{p}(1)
.

Because
\Delta_{(\gamma,\theta)}f(x;\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{0}^{*},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{0}^{*})
is identical to
\Delta_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}f(x;\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)
, a standard analysis gives
2[L_{0,n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{0},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{0})-L_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{*}_{0},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{0})]=\max_{\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}B_{n}(\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}})+o_{p}(1)
.
Note that the possible values of both
\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
and
\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
approach
\mathbb{R}^{q}
. Therefore, in view of (
68
) and (
69
), we can write equation (
67
) as

LR_{n}=\max_{\alpha\in[c_{1},1-c_{1}]}C_{n}(\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{\alpha},\alpha))+o_{p}(1),

(70)

where
\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{\alpha}
is as defined in (
63
).

The asymptotic distribution of
LR_{n}
follows from applying Theorem 3(c) of
(
Andrews, 1999
, p. 1362)
to (
68
) and (
70
).
First, Assumption 2 of
Andrews, 1999
holds because Assumption 2* of
Andrews, 1999
holds because of Proposition 2(a).
Second, Assumption 3 of
Andrews, 1999
holds with
B_{T}=n^{1/2}
and
T=n
because
\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}n}\overset{d}{\to}\boldsymbol{S}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}\sim N(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}})

and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}
is non-singular.
Assumption 4 of
Andrews, 1999
holds from part (a).
Assumption 5 of
Andrews, 1999
follows from Assumption 5* and Lemma 3 of
Andrews, 1999
with
b_{T}=n^{1/2}
because
\alpha(1-\alpha)v(\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})
is locally equal to
\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
.
Therefore, it follows from Theorem 3(c) of
Andrews, 1999
that
C_{n}(\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}},\alpha))\overset{d}{\to}(\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
,
where
\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is defined by (
25
).

∎

Proof of Proposition
5
.

Under
H_{2,0}
, we obtain
\vartheta_{M_{0}+1}\in\Upsilon_{2h}^{*}
,

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}[\{\nabla_{\alpha_{h}}\log f_{M_{0}+1}(\boldsymbol{W}_{i},\vartheta_{M_{0}+1})\}^{2}]\\
&=\int\frac{\{f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h})-f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{M_{0}}^{*})\}^{2}}{\sum_{j=1}^{M_{0}}\alpha_{j}^{*}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{*})}d\boldsymbol{w}\\
&=\int\frac{\{f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h})\}^{2}}{\sum_{j=1}^{M_{0}}\alpha_{j}^{*}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{*})}d\boldsymbol{w}+\int\frac{\{f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{M_{0}}^{*})\}^{2}}{\sum_{j=1}^{M_{0}}\alpha_{j}^{*}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{*})}d\boldsymbol{w}-2\int\frac{f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h})f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{M_{0}}^{*})}{\sum_{j=1}^{M_{0}}\alpha_{j}^{*}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{*})}d\boldsymbol{w}.\end{split}

(71)

The latter two terms on the right-hand side of (
71
) are bounded because

f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{M_{0}}^{*})/\sum_{j=1}^{M_{0}}\alpha_{j}^{*}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{*})\leq(1/\alpha_{M_{0}}^{*})
for any
\boldsymbol{w}
and
f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta})
integrates to one.
Therefore, the left-hand side of (
71
) goes to infinity if and only if the first term on the right-hand side of (
71
) goes to infinity.

Because
\max_{j}\alpha_{j}\leq\sum_{j}^{M_{0}}\alpha_{j}\leq M_{0}\max_{j}\alpha_{j}
, we obtain

\frac{1}{M_{0}}\frac{\{f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h})\}^{2}}{\max_{j}\{\alpha_{j}^{*}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{*})\}}\leq\frac{\{f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h})\}^{2}}{\sum_{j=1}^{M_{0}}\alpha_{j}^{*}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{*})}\leq\frac{\{f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h})\}^{2}}{\max_{j}\{\alpha_{j}^{*}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{*})\}}.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\sigma_{M_{0}}^{*}=\max\{\sigma_{1}^{*},\ldots,\sigma_{M_{0}}^{*}\}
and that the maximum is unique.
We focus on models without covariates because the law of iterated expectations implies that the stated result also holds for models with covariates if it holds for models without covariates.

We first prove the case for the component-specific density function
f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta})=\prod_{t=1}^{T}f(y_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})
, where
f(y_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})
is given by (
2
) or (
3
) with
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}=\boldsymbol{0}
. Under the normal density function (
2
) without covariates, there exists a sufficiently large but finite positive constant
B
, such that
\max_{j}\{\alpha_{j}^{*}f(\boldsymbol{w},\mu_{j}^{*},\sigma_{j}^{2})\}=\alpha_{M_{0}}^{*}f(\boldsymbol{w},\mu_{M_{0}}^{*},\sigma_{M_{0}}^{2})
when
|y_{t}|>B
for all
t=1,\ldots,T
.
Note that

\begin{split}\frac{\{f(\boldsymbol{w};\mu_{h},\sigma_{h})\}^{2}}{f(\boldsymbol{w};\mu_{M_{0}}^{*},\sigma_{M_{0}}^{*})}&=\prod_{t=1}^{T}\frac{\sigma_{M_{0}}^{*}}{(2\pi)^{1/2}\sigma_{h}^{2}}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{\sigma_{h}^{2}}(y_{t}-\mu_{h})^{2}+\frac{1}{2(\sigma_{M_{0}}^{*})^{2}}(y_{t}-\mu_{M_{0}}^{*})^{2}\right\}\\
&=\left(\frac{\sigma_{M_{0}}^{*}}{(2\pi)^{1/2}\sigma_{h}^{2}}\right)^{T}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{\sigma_{h}^{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(y_{t}-\mu_{h})^{2}+\frac{1}{2(\sigma_{M_{0}}^{*})^{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(y_{t}-\mu_{M_{0}}^{*})^{2}\right\}.\end{split}

(72)

Then, the integral of the right-hand side of (
72
) over
|y_{t}|\geq B
for
t=1,...,T
is infinite if
\sigma_{h}^{2}/\sigma_{M_{0}}^{2*}>2
, and the stated result holds.

When
f(y_{t};\boldsymbol{\theta})
is given by normal mixture density (
3
), suppose that
\mu_{j1}<\mu_{j2}<\cdots<\mu_{{\cal{K}}}
for all
j=1,....,M_{0}
. Then, there exists a constant
B
such that, when
y_{t}>B
for all
t=1,\ldots,T
, we have
\max_{j}\{\alpha_{j}^{*}f(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{j})\}=\alpha_{M_{0}}^{*}f(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{M_{0}})
and

\frac{1}{\sigma_{j}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{y_{t}-\mu_{{\cal{K}}}}{\sigma_{j}}\right)^{2}\right)\geq\sum_{k=1}^{{\cal{K}}}\tau_{jk}\frac{1}{\sigma_{j}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{y_{t}-\mu_{jk}}{\sigma_{j}}\right)^{2}\right)\geq\frac{1}{\sigma_{j}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{y_{t}-\mu_{j1}}{\sigma_{j}}\right)^{2}\right).

(73)

Then, it follows that

\frac{\{f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h})\}^{2}}{f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{M_{0}}^{*})}\geq\left(\frac{\sigma_{M_{0}}^{*}}{(2\pi)^{1/2}\sigma_{h}^{2}}\right)^{T}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{\sigma_{h}^{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(y_{t}-\mu_{1h})^{2}+\frac{1}{2(\sigma_{M_{0}}^{*})^{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(y_{t}-\mu_{M_{0}K}^{*})^{2}\right\},

where the integral of the right-hand side over
y_{t}>B
for
t=1,...,T
is infinite if
\sigma_{h}^{2}/\sigma_{M_{0}}^{2*}>2
as in (
72
).

Next, for dynamic panel models, suppose that
f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta})=f_{1}(y_{1};\boldsymbol{\theta})\prod_{t=2}^{T}f(y_{t}|y_{t-1};\boldsymbol{\theta})
with
f_{1}(y_{1};\boldsymbol{\theta})
and
f(y_{t}|y_{t-1};\boldsymbol{\theta})
given in
(
9
) with
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1,j}=\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}=\boldsymbol{0}
. Fix the value of
\{y_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T-1}
. Then,

\displaystyle\frac{\{f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h})\}^{2}}{f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{M_{0}}^{*})}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{f_{1}(y_{1};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h})^{2}\prod_{t=2}^{T-1}f(y_{t}|y_{t-1};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h})^{2}}{f_{1}(y_{1};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{M_{0}}^{*})\prod_{t=2}^{T-1}f(y_{t}|y_{t-1};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{M_{0}}^{*})}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{\sigma_{M_{0}}^{*}}{(2\pi)^{1/2}\sigma_{h}^{2}}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{\sigma_{h}^{2}}(y_{T}-\mu_{h}-\rho_{h}y_{T-1})^{2}+\frac{1}{2(\sigma_{M_{0}}^{*})^{2}}(y_{T}-\mu_{M_{0}}^{*}-\rho_{M_{0}}^{*}y_{T-1})^{2}\right\},

where the integral of the right-hand side of this equation over
|y_{T}|\geq B
is infinite for a sufficiently large
B
if
\sigma_{h}^{2}/\sigma_{M_{0}}^{2*}>2
. Therefore,
E\left[\left.\frac{\{f(\boldsymbol{W};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h})\}^{2}}{f(\boldsymbol{W};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{M_{0}}^{*})}\right|\{Y_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T-1}=\{y_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T-1}\right]=\infty
, and the stated result follows from the law of iterated expectations. The case for
f_{1}(y_{1};\boldsymbol{\theta})
and
f(y_{t}|y_{t-1};\boldsymbol{\theta})
given in
(
10
) can be proven analogously, and hence we omit it.

∎

Proof of Proposition
6
.

Collect the score vector for testing
H_{0,1h}
for
h=1,\ldots,M_{0}
into one vector as

\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}(\boldsymbol{W})=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{W})\\
\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{W})\end{pmatrix},\ \text{ where }\underset{(M_{0}+p+q+1)\times 1}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{W})}=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{W})\\
\boldsymbol{s}_{(\boldsymbol{\nu})}(\boldsymbol{W})\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{ and }\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{W})=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{s}^{1}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{W})\\
\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{s}^{M_{0}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{W})\end{pmatrix},

(74)

where

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{W})&=\begin{pmatrix}f(\boldsymbol{W};\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{1})-f(\boldsymbol{W};\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{M_{0}})\\
\vdots\\
f(\boldsymbol{W};\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{M_{0}-1})-f(\boldsymbol{W};\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{M_{0}})\end{pmatrix}\Bigg/f_{M_{0}}(\boldsymbol{W};\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M_{0}}^{*}),\\
\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{W})&=\sum_{j=1}^{M_{0}}\alpha_{j}^{*}\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}f(\boldsymbol{W};\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{j})}{f_{M_{0}}(\boldsymbol{W};\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M_{0}}^{*})},\ \ \text{and}\ \ \boldsymbol{s}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{W})=\frac{\widetilde{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h}^{\top}}f(\boldsymbol{W};\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{h})}{f_{M_{0}}(\boldsymbol{W};\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M_{0}}^{*})}\quad\text{for $h=1,2,...,M_{0}$},\end{split}

(75)

with

\widetilde{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}_{h}}f(\boldsymbol{W};\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{h}):=(c_{11}\nabla_{\theta_{h1}\theta_{h1}}f^{*},...,c_{qq}\nabla_{\theta_{hq}\theta_{hq}}f^{*},c_{12}\nabla_{\theta_{h1}\theta_{h2}}f^{*},...,c_{(q-1)q}\nabla_{\theta_{hq-1}\theta_{hq}}f^{*})^{\top}

for
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h}:=(\theta_{h1},\theta_{h2},\theta_{h3},...,\theta_{hq})^{\top}:=(\mu_{h},\sigma_{h}^{2},\beta_{h1},...,\beta_{hq-2})^{\top}
and
c_{jk}=1/2
for
j\neq k
and
c_{jk}=1
for
j=k
.
Define

\begin{split}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}:=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\tilde{s}}(\boldsymbol{W})\boldsymbol{\tilde{s}}(\boldsymbol{W})^{\top}],\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}:=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\tilde{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{W})\boldsymbol{\tilde{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{W})^{\top}],\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\eta}}:=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\tilde{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{W})\boldsymbol{\tilde{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{W})^{\top}],\\
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{\lambda}}:=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\top},\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda\lambda}}:=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\tilde{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{W})\boldsymbol{\tilde{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{W})^{\top}],\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}_{\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}}:=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda\lambda}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda\eta}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{-1}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta\lambda}}.\end{split}

(76)

Then, the asymptotic distribution of the normalized score function is given by

\tilde{\boldsymbol{S}}_{n}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}(\boldsymbol{W}_{i})\overset{d}{\to}\tilde{\boldsymbol{S}}\sim N(\boldsymbol{0},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}),

where, in view of (
74
),
\tilde{\boldsymbol{S}}
may be partitioned as
\tilde{\boldsymbol{S}}=(\tilde{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\top},\tilde{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\top})^{\top}
with
n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{W}_{i})\overset{d}{\to}\tilde{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
and
n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{W}_{i})\overset{d}{\to}\tilde{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}:=(\boldsymbol{{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{M_{0}})^{\top}:=\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\lambda}}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\sim N(0,\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}})
be a
\mathbb{R}^{M_{0}(q)(q+1)/2}
-valued random vector. For
h=1,2,...,M_{0}
, define
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{h}:=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{h}(\boldsymbol{{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{h})^{\top}]
and
\boldsymbol{{G}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{h}:=({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{h})^{-1}\boldsymbol{{S}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{h}
.

Define
\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
analogously to
\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
as

\begin{split}r^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})=\inf_{{\boldsymbol{t}}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\in\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}r^{h}({\boldsymbol{t}}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}});\quad r^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}({\boldsymbol{t}}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}):=({\boldsymbol{t}}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}-\boldsymbol{{G}}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}})^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{h}({\boldsymbol{t}}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}-\boldsymbol{{G}}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}})\quad\text{for $h=1,2,...,M_{0}$}.\end{split}

(77)

The local quadratic-form approximation of the log-likelihood function
LR^{M_{0},h}_{n}
around
\Theta^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M_{0}+1},1h}\subset\Theta_{\vartheta_{M_{0}+1}}
has an identical structure to the approximation that we derive in Section
5
in testing
H_{01}
in the test of homogeneity. Consequently, we can show that
LR^{M_{0},h}_{n}\overset{d}{\to}(\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
. Then, given (
31
), the asymptotic null distribution of the LRTS for testing
H_{01}
is given by the maximum over
(\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
s for
h=1,2,...,M_{0}
. We now proceed with the proof.

For
h=1,\ldots,M_{0}
, let
\mathcal{N}_{h}^{*}\subset\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M_{0}+1}}(\boldsymbol{c})
be a sufficiently small closed neighborhood of
\Theta^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M_{0}+1},1h}
such that
\alpha_{h},\alpha_{h+1}>0
holds and
\Theta^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M_{0}+1},1k}\not\subset\mathcal{N}_{h}^{*}
if
k\neq h
. Consider the following one-to-one reparameterization from the
(M_{0}+1)
-component model parameter
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M_{0}+1}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{M_{0}},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}_{h},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}_{h+1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}_{M_{0}+1})^{\top}
.
Similar to (
22
), the one-to-one reparameterization for testing the null hypothesis
H_{0,1h}
is given by

\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{h}\\
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{h}\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h+1}\\
\tau\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h}+(1-\tau)\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h+1}\end{pmatrix}\text{ so that }\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h}\\
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h+1}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\nu}+(1-\tau)\boldsymbol{\lambda}\\
\boldsymbol{\nu}-\tau\boldsymbol{\lambda}\end{pmatrix},

and
\alpha_{j}
is reparameterized for
j=1,2,...,M_{0}
as

\displaystyle(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{h-1},\pi_{h},\pi_{h+1},\ldots,\pi_{M_{0}-1})

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{h-1},(\alpha_{h}+\alpha_{h+1}),\alpha_{h+2},\ldots,\alpha_{M_{0}})

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle={\alpha_{h}}/({\alpha_{h}+\alpha_{h+1}})

so that
\pi_{h}=\alpha_{h}+\alpha_{h+1}
and
\pi_{M_{0}}=1-\sum_{j=1}^{M_{0}-1}\pi_{j}
.

Collect the reparameterized parameters except
\tau
as

\boldsymbol{\psi}_{h,\tau}=(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\top},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\top}_{h})^{\top}\quad\text{with}\quad\boldsymbol{\eta}=(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{M_{0}-1},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}_{h-1},\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\top}_{h},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}_{h+2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}_{M_{0}+1})^{\top}.

In the reparameterized model, the null restriction
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{h+1}
implied by
H_{0,1h}
holds if and only if
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{h}=0
. Under
H_{0,1h}
, we have
\lambda_{h}^{*}=0
and

\eta^{*}=(\alpha_{1}^{*},...,\alpha_{M_{0}-1}^{*},(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{1})^{\top},\ldots,(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}_{M_{0}})^{\top})^{\top}
.
Define the log-likelihood under the reparameterized parameters as

f_{M_{0}+1}^{h}(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\psi}_{h,\tau},\tau)=\pi_{h}g^{h}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{h,\tau},\tau)+\sum_{j=1}^{h-1}\pi_{j}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j})+\sum_{j=h}^{M_{0}}\pi_{j+1}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j+1}),

where
g^{h}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{h,\tau},\tau)
is defined similarly to (
62
) as

g^{h}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{h,\tau},\tau)=\tau f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\nu}_{h}+(1-\tau)\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{h})+(1-\tau)f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\nu}_{h}-\tau\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{h}).

(78)

Define the local MLE of
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{h,\tau}
by

\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}_{h,\tau}:=\argmax_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{h,\tau}\in\mathcal{N}_{h}^{*}}L^{h}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{h,\tau},\tau),

(79)

where
L_{n}^{h}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{h,\tau},\tau):=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log g^{h}(\boldsymbol{W}_{i};\boldsymbol{\psi}_{h,\tau},\tau)
.
Because
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{h,\tau}^{*}
is the only parameter value in
\mathcal{N}_{h}^{*}
that generates the true density,
\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}_{h,\tau}-\boldsymbol{\psi}_{h,\tau}^{*}=o_{p}(1)
follows.

Define the LRTS for testing
H_{0,1h}
as
LR_{n}^{M_{0},h}:=\max_{\tau\in[c_{1},1-c_{1}]}2(L_{n}^{h}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{h,\tau},\tau)-L_{0,n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}}))
. Then, in view of (
31
), the stated result holds if

(LR_{n}^{M_{0},1},\ldots,LR_{n}^{M_{0},M_{0}})^{\top}\overset{d}{\to}(\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}^{1}_{\lambda})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}^{1}_{\eta,\lambda}(\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}^{1}_{\lambda}),\ldots,(\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}^{M_{0}}_{\lambda})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}^{M_{0}}_{\eta,\lambda}(\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}^{M_{0}}_{\lambda}))^{\top}.

(80)

Observe that
L^{h}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{h,\tau},\tau)-L^{h}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}_{h,\tau},\tau)
admits the same expansion as

L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}},\alpha)-L_{n}(\boldsymbol{{\psi}}^{*},\alpha)
in (
66
) and (
68
) when

(\alpha,\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha),\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda},\alpha),\boldsymbol{S}_{n},\boldsymbol{G}_{n},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n},R_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha))
is replaced with

(\tau,\boldsymbol{t}^{h}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h},\tau),\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{h}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{h},\tau),\boldsymbol{S}_{n}^{h},\boldsymbol{G}_{n}^{h},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}^{h},R_{n}^{h}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h},\tau))
, where
(\boldsymbol{S}_{n}^{h},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}^{h})
is defined similarly to
(\boldsymbol{S}_{n},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n})
but

(\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda\lambda}})
is replaced with
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}},{\boldsymbol{s}}^{h}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda\lambda}})
and
\boldsymbol{G}_{n}^{h}:=(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}^{h})^{-1}\boldsymbol{S}_{n}^{h}
. Applying the proof of Lemma
4
, we have
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{n}^{h}\overset{d}{\to}{\boldsymbol{S}}^{h}\sim N(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}^{h})
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}^{h}\overset{p}{\to}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}^{h}
. Then, (
80
) follows from the proof of Lemma
4
and Proposition
4
for each local MLE when
(\boldsymbol{G}_{n},\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}})
is replaced with
(\boldsymbol{G}_{n}^{h},\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{h},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{h})
and the results are collected; note that
(\boldsymbol{S}_{n}^{1},...,\boldsymbol{S}_{n}^{M_{0}})\overset{d}{\to}(\boldsymbol{S}^{1},...,\boldsymbol{S}^{M_{0}})
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7
.

We first prove that when
M<M_{0}
,
\Pr(LR_{n}^{M}>\hat{c}^{M}_{1-q_{n}})\rightarrow 1
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Under Assumption
5
, by the standard consistency proof
(
Newey and McFadden, 1994
, e.g., Theorem 2.5 of)
give
\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M}\overset{p}{\rightarrow}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}^{*}
for
M\leq M_{0}
. Furthermore, it follows from the argument in Theorem 3.2 of
White, 1982
that, for
M\leq M_{0}
,

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}^{*})\overset{p}{\rightarrow}N(0,A^{M}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}^{*})^{-1}B^{M}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}^{*})A^{M}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}^{*})^{-1}).

(81)

Then, from (
81
) and the mean value expansion, we have
Q_{n}^{M}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M})-Q^{M}({\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M}^{*})=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
and

\displaystyle\frac{LR_{n}^{M}}{n}

\displaystyle:=2\left(Q_{n}^{M+1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M+1})-Q_{n}^{M}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M})\right)=2\left(Q^{M+1}({\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M+1}^{*})-Q^{M}({\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M}^{*})\right)+o_{p}(1)

for
M=1,2,...,M_{0}-1
.

Because
Q^{M+1}({\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M+1}^{*})-Q^{M}({\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M}^{*})>0
by Assumption
5
(f),
{LR_{n}^{M}}/{n}\rightarrow\infty
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. By Lemma
5
,
-n^{-1}\log q_{n}=o(1)
and
\hat{c}^{M}_{1-q_{n}}-c^{M}_{1-q_{n}}=o_{p}(1)
implies that
n^{-1}\hat{c}^{M}_{1-q_{n}}=o_{p}(1)
. Therefore, when
M<M_{0}
,
we have
\Pr(LR_{n}^{M}>\hat{c}^{M}_{1-q_{n}})=\Pr(LR_{n}^{M}/n>\hat{c}^{M}_{1-q_{n}}/n)\rightarrow 1
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

When
M=M_{0}
, because
LR_{n}^{M_{0}}=O_{p}(1)
by Proposition
6
and
\hat{c}^{M}_{1-q_{n}}\rightarrow\infty
by
q_{n}=o(1)
,
\Pr(LR_{n}^{M_{0}}>\hat{c}^{M_{0}}_{1-q_{n}})\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
8
.

We first prove that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Pr(\widehat{M}_{PL}<M_{0})=0
. To show
\Pr(\hat{M}_{PL}<M_{0})\to 0
, it suffices to prove that for each
M<M_{0}
,

\Pr\bigl(p\ell_{n}^{M}>p\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}\bigr)\;\to\;0.

By definition,

\frac{p\ell_{n}^{M}-p\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}}{n}=(Q_{n}^{M}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M})-Q_{n}^{M_{0}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}}))-\frac{p_{n,k_{M}}-p_{n,k_{M_{0}}}}{n}.

(82)

From
p_{n,k}=o(n)
in Assumption
6
(c),

\frac{p_{n,k_{M}}-p_{n,k_{M_{0}}}}{n}\rightarrow 0.

(83)

By the union bound and the triangle inequality, for any
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\Pr\Bigl(

\displaystyle\left|Q_{n}^{M}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M})-Q_{n}^{M_{0}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})-\Bigl(Q^{M}({\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M}^{*})-Q^{M_{0}}({\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}}^{*})\Bigr)\right|>4\epsilon\Bigr)

is at most

\displaystyle\Pr\Bigl(\left|Q^{M}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M})-Q^{M}({\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M}^{*})\right|>\epsilon\Bigr)+\Pr\Bigl(\left|Q^{M_{0}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})-Q^{M_{0}}({\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}}^{*})\right|>\epsilon\Bigr)

(84)

\displaystyle\quad+\,\Pr\Bigl(\left|Q_{n}^{M}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M})-Q^{M}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M})\right|>\epsilon\Bigr)+\Pr\Bigl(\left|Q_{n}^{M_{0}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})-Q^{M_{0}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})\right|>\epsilon\Bigr).

(85)

The consistency of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}}
, together with the Continuous Mapping Theorem, implies that the two terms in (
84
) converge to
0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

For small
\delta>0
, let
B(\delta,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}^{*})
and
B(\delta,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M_{0}}^{*})
be open balls in the parameter spaces
\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}}
and
\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M_{0}}}
, respectively. Since
\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}}
are consistent, with probability approaching one, the two terms in (
85
) are bounded by

\Pr\Bigl(\sup_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}\in B(\delta,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}^{*})}\left|Q_{n}^{M}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M})-Q^{M}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M})\right|>\epsilon\Bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad\Pr\Bigl(\sup_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M_{0}}\in B(\delta,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M_{0}}^{*})}\left|Q_{n}^{M_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M_{0}})-Q^{M_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M_{0}})\right|>\epsilon\Bigr).

By applying Lemma 2.4 of
Newey and McFadden, 1994
under Assumption
5
(b), and noting that
\log g_{M}(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M})
is continuous at each
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}
and the envelope function
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}\in B(\delta,\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M}^{*})}|\log g_{M}(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{M})|
has finite expectation for
M\leq M_{0}
, these two probabilities converge to 0 by the Uniform Law of Large Numbers. Thus, we obtain

Q_{n}^{M}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M})-Q_{n}^{M_{0}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}Q^{M}(\theta_{M}^{*})-Q^{M_{0}}(\theta_{M_{0}}^{*}).

(86)

Therefore, from (
82
), (
83
), and (
86
), and in view of Assumptions
5
(f) and
6
(c), we have

\frac{p\ell_{n}^{M}-p\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}}{n}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}Q^{M}(\theta_{M}^{*})-Q^{M_{0}}(\theta_{M_{0}}^{*})<0.

Hence,
\Pr(p\ell_{n}^{M}>p\ell_{n}^{M_{0}})\to 0
for
M<M_{0}
, which proves that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Pr(\hat{M}_{PL}<M_{0})=0.

(87)

We proceed to prove that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Pr(\widehat{M}_{PL}>M_{0})=0
by showing that, for
M=M_{0}+1,...,\bar{M}
,

\Pr(p\ell_{n}^{M}>p\ell_{n}^{M_{0}})\rightarrow 0\quad\text{as $n\rightarrow\infty$}.

By definition of
p\ell_{n}^{M}
, we have

\Pr(p\ell_{n}^{M}>p\ell_{n}^{M_{0}})=\Pr\left(\frac{\ell_{n}^{M}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M})-\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})}{p_{n,M_{0}}}+1-\frac{p_{n,k_{M}}}{p_{n,k_{M_{0}}}}>0\right).

For any
\epsilon>0
,
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Pr\left(\frac{\ell_{n}^{M}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M})-\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})}{p_{n,M_{0}}}>\epsilon\right)=0
because
p_{n,M_{0}}\rightarrow\infty
and
\ell_{n}^{M}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M})-\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})=O_{p}(1)
by Assumptions
6
(b) and
7
, respectively. Therefore,

\frac{\ell_{n}^{M}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M})-\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})}{p_{n,M_{0}}}+1-\frac{p_{n,k_{M}}}{p_{n,k_{M_{0}}}}\overset{p}{\rightarrow}1-\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{p_{n,k_{M}}}{p_{n,k_{M_{0}}}},

which is strictly negative by Assumption
6
(d). Thus,
\Pr(p\ell_{n}^{M}>p\ell_{n}^{M_{0}})\rightarrow 0\quad\text{as $n\rightarrow\infty$}
, and
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Pr(\widehat{M}_{PL}>M_{0})=0
follows.

∎

Proof of Proposition
9
.

Because
\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})-\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}({\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}}^{*})=O_{p}(1)
under standard regularity conditions in Assumption
5
, Assumption
7
holds when
\ell_{n}^{M}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M})-\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}({\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}}^{*})=O_{p}(1)
in view of
\ell_{n}^{M}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M})-\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})=(\ell_{n}^{M}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M})-\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}({\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}}^{*}))-(\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})-\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}({\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}}^{*}))
.
Because a consistent MLE is in
A_{\varepsilon_{n}}(\eta)
defined in Appendix
A.2
, Propositions
3
and
A.3
imply that
\ell_{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{M}^{j},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{j})-\ell_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}^{j*},\boldsymbol{\tau}^{j})=O_{p}(1)
, and the stated result holds.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1
.

It is straightforward to verify that the penalty function
p_{n,k}=\frac{k}{2}\log(n)
satisfies Assumption
6
. Thus, result (a) directly follows from Proposition
9
.
For (b), Assumption
6
(c) holds for AIC penalty. Then, repeating the argument in the proof of Proposition
8
up to (
87
) proves that
p\ell^{M_{0}}_{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})>\max\left\{p\ell_{n}^{1}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{1}),...,p\ell_{n}^{M_{0}-1}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}-1})\right\}
holds with probability approaching one as
n\rightarrow\infty
. This implies that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Pr\left(\widehat{M}_{PL}>M_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Pr\left(p\ell^{M_{0}+1}_{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}+1})>\max\left\{p\ell_{n}^{1}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{1}),...,p\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Pr\left(p\ell^{M_{0}+1}_{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}+1})>p\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Pr\left(2\left[\ell^{M_{0}+1}_{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}+1})-\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})\right]>2(k_{M_{0}+1}-k_{M_{0}})\right),

(88)

where

k_{M_{0}}
and
k_{M_{0}+1}
are the number of parameters for
M_{0}
and
(M_{0}+1)
components model. Since the term
2\left[\ell^{M_{0}+1}_{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}+1})-\ell_{n}^{M_{0}}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{M_{0}})\right]
is
O_{p}(1)
and converges in distribution as specified in Proposition
6
, and because
k_{M_{0}}
and
k_{M_{0}+1}
are finite, it follows from (
88
) that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Pr\left(\widehat{M}_{PL}>M_{0}\right)>0,

and part (b) follows.
∎

Proof of Proposition
A.1
.

The part (a) can be proven similar to Proposition
3
, and thus omitted.

The proof for part (b) closely follows Theorem 2(b) in
Andrews, 2001
. For each
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(c_{1})
, we approximate the log-likelihood function
\ell_{n}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\lambda},\alpha)
around the true parameter values
(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},0)
using the partial derivative with respect to
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}
and the right partial derivative with respect to
\alpha
to obtain

\displaystyle 2\{\ell_{n}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\lambda},\alpha)

\displaystyle-\ell_{n}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\lambda},0)\}=\boldsymbol{G}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\boldsymbol{G}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})

\displaystyle-\left[\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2},\alpha)-\boldsymbol{G}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right]^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}\left[\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2},\alpha)-\boldsymbol{G}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right]+R_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\lambda},\alpha),

(89)

where
R_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\lambda},\alpha)
is a remainder term, and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
,
\boldsymbol{G}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
, and
\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2},\alpha)
are defined as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}):=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{s}}(\boldsymbol{W}_{i};\boldsymbol{\lambda}){\boldsymbol{s}}(\boldsymbol{W}_{i};\boldsymbol{\lambda})^{\top},\ \boldsymbol{G}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}):=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})^{-1}\boldsymbol{S}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),\ \text{and}\ \boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2},\alpha):=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\\
\alpha\end{pmatrix}

with
\boldsymbol{S}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{s}}(\boldsymbol{W}_{i};\boldsymbol{\lambda})
and
{\boldsymbol{s}}(\boldsymbol{W}_{i};\boldsymbol{\lambda})
defined in (
40
).
(\theta,\pi)
and
(B_{T},D\ell_{T}(\theta_{0},\pi),\mathcal{J}_{T\pi},Z_{T\pi})
in
Andrews, 2001
correspond to our
((\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}^{\top},\alpha)^{\top},\boldsymbol{\lambda})
and
(n^{1/2},n^{1/2}\boldsymbol{S}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),\boldsymbol{G}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))
.

We prove the stated result by applying Theorem 2(b) of
Andrews, 2001
to (
89
).
(\beta,\delta,\pi)
and
(B_{T},G_{\pi},\mathcal{J}_{\pi},Z_{\pi},Z_{\beta\pi})
in
Andrews, 2001
correspond to our
(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\lambda})
and
(n^{1/2},\boldsymbol{S}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),\boldsymbol{G}_{\alpha,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))
, where
\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}):=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})^{-1}\boldsymbol{S}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
,
\boldsymbol{G}_{\alpha,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}):=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})^{-1}\boldsymbol{S}_{\alpha,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
, and
\psi
in
Andrews, 2001
does not exist in our setting. The stated result then follows because
\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{w})
is identical to the score of the one-component model and
\hat{\lambda}_{\beta\pi}^{\prime}(H\mathcal{J}_{*\pi}^{-1}H^{\prime})^{-1}\hat{\lambda}_{\beta\pi}
in Theorem 2(b) of
Andrews, 2001
is distributed as
(\max\{0,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\alpha.\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{S}_{\alpha.\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\})^{2}
. We proceed to verify the assumptions of Theorem 2(b) of
Andrews, 2001
(hereafter, A-Assumptions
2^{2^{*}}
, 3-5, 7, and 8). A-Assumption
2^{2^{*}}
(a)(b) follow from our Assumption
A.1
(a)(b). A-Assumption
2^{2^{*}}
(c) holds because our Assumption
A.1
(c)(d) and the uniform law of large numbers imply that
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(c_{1})}||\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})||\rightarrow_{p}0
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
is continuous.

A-Assumption 3 follows from Theorem 10.2 of
Pollard, 1990
if (i)
\widetilde{\Theta}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is totally bounded, (ii) the finite dimensional distributions of
\boldsymbol{G}_{n}(\cdot)
converge to those of
\boldsymbol{G}(\cdot)
, and (iii)
\{\boldsymbol{G}_{n}(\cdot):n\geq 1\}
is stochastically equicontinuous. Condition (i) follows from the compactness of
\widetilde{\Theta}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
while conditions (ii) follows from Assumption
A.1
(b)(c) and the multivariate CLT. Condition (iii) can be verified by our Assumption
A.1
(b)(c) and Theorem 2 of
Andrews, 1994
because
\nabla_{(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}^{\top},\alpha)^{\top}}\log g_{2}(\cdot;\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\lambda},\alpha)
are Lipschitz functions indexed by a finite dimensional parameter
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
by Assumption
A.1
(b).

A-Assumption 4 follows from Lemma 1 of
Andrews, 2001
because, for each
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\in\widetilde{\Theta}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(c_{1})
,
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),\tilde{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}))=\arg\max_{(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2},\alpha)\in\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}\times[0,1/2]}\ell_{n}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2},\boldsymbol{\lambda},\alpha)
converges to
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}^{*},0)
in probability from the standard consistency proof. A-Assumption 5 holds because (i) the set
[0,1]
equals a nonnegative half-line locally around
0
, and (ii)
\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}^{*}
is locally equal to
{\mathbb{R}}^{\text{dim}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2})}
. A-Assumption 7(a) is not relevant for our problem. A-Assumptions 7(b) and 8 follow from our proof of A-Assumption 5.

∎

Proof of Proposition
A.2
.

For notational brevity, we drop the superscript
j
from
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}^{j}
,
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{j}
,
\boldsymbol{s}_{i}^{j}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}^{j}
.
By using the Taylor expansion of
2\log(1+x)=2x-x^{2}(1+o(1))
for small
x
, we have uniformly
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{c/\sqrt{n}}
,

\ell_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M},\boldsymbol{\tau})-\ell_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}^{*},\boldsymbol{\tau})=2\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log(1+h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i})=nP_{n}(2h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}-[1+o_{p}(1)]h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}^{2}),

(90)

where
h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}:=\sqrt{l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}}-1
.
The stated result holds if we show that

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{c/\sqrt{n}}}\left|nP_{n}(h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}^{2})-n\boldsymbol{t}^{\top}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}/4\right|=o_{p}(1)\quad\text{and}

(91)

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{c/\sqrt{n}}}|nP_{n}(h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i})-\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}^{\top}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}\nu_{n}(\boldsymbol{s}_{i})/2+n\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}\boldsymbol{t}^{\top}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}/8|=o_{p}(1),

(92)

because then the right-hand side of (
90
) is equal to
\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}^{\top}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}\nu_{n}(\boldsymbol{s}_{i})-\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}\boldsymbol{t}^{\top}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}/2+o_{p}(1)
uniformly in
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{c/\sqrt{n}}
.

We first show (
91
). Let

m_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}i}:=l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}-1=\boldsymbol{t}^{\top}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}\boldsymbol{s}_{i}+r_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}.

Observe that

\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{c/\sqrt{n}}}|m_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}i}|=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{c/\sqrt{n}}}|\boldsymbol{t}^{\top}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}\boldsymbol{s}_{i}+r_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}|=o_{p}(1),

(93)

from Assumptions
A.2
(a) and (c) and Lemma
7
. Write
4P_{n}(h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}^{2})
as

4P_{n}(h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}^{2})=P_{n}\left(\frac{4(l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}-1)^{2}}{(\sqrt{l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}}+1)^{2}}\right)=P_{n}(l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}-1)^{2}-P_{n}\left((l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}-1)^{3}\frac{(\sqrt{l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}}+3)}{(\sqrt{l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}}+1)^{3}}\right).

(94)

From (
52
),

\displaystyle P_{n}(l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}-1)^{2}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{t}^{\top}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}P_{n}(\boldsymbol{s}_{i}\boldsymbol{s}_{i}^{\prime})\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}+\zeta_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}n},

(95)

where
\zeta_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}n}:=2\boldsymbol{t}^{\top}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}P_{n}[\boldsymbol{s}_{i}r_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}]+P_{n}(r_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i})^{2}
. It follows from Assumptions
A.2
(a)(b)(c) and
(E|XY|)^{2}\leq E|X|^{2}E|Y|^{2}
that, uniformly in
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
,

|\zeta_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}n}|=O_{p}(||\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}||^{2}||\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}-\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}^{*}||)+O_{p}(n^{-1}||\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}||||\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}-\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}^{*}||)+O_{p}(n^{-1}||\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}-\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}^{*}||^{2}).

(96)

Then, (
91
) holds because
||P_{n}(\boldsymbol{s}_{i}\boldsymbol{s}_{i}^{\prime})-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}||=o_{p}(1)
and the second term on the right-hand side of (
94
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{c/\sqrt{n}}}P_{n}\left[|m_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}i}|^{3}\right]\leq o_{p}(1)\sup_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{c/\sqrt{n}}}P_{n}\left[m_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}i}^{2}\right]=o_{p}(n^{-1}),

from
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{c/\sqrt{n}}}\frac{(\sqrt{l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}}+3)}{(\sqrt{l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}}+1)^{3}}\leq\mathcal{C}
, (
93
), and
P_{n}(m_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}i}^{2})=\boldsymbol{t}^{\top}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}+o_{p}(||\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}||^{2})
.

We proceed to show (
92
). Consider the following expansion of
h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}
:

h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}=(l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}-1)/2-h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}^{2}/2=(\boldsymbol{t}^{\top}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau}}\boldsymbol{s}_{i}+r_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i})/2-h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\tau},i}^{2}/2.

(97)

Then, (
92
) follows from (
91
), (
97
), and Assumptions
A.2
(d), and the stated result follows.
∎

Proof of Proposition
A.3
.

For brevity, we drop the superscript
M
from
\boldsymbol{s}_{i}^{M}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}^{M}
.
Define
h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}:=\sqrt{l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}}-1
.
For part (a), it follows from
\log(1+x)\leq x
and
h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}=(l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}-1)/2-h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}^{2}/2
(see (
97
)) that

\ell_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M},\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ell_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}^{*},\boldsymbol{\alpha})=2\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log(1+h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i})\leq 2nP_{n}(h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i})=\sqrt{n}\nu_{n}(l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}-1)-nP_{n}(h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}^{2}).

(98)

Observe that
h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}^{2}=(l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}-1)^{2}/(\sqrt{l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}}+1)^{2}\geq\mathbb{I}\{l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}\leq\kappa\}(l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}-1)^{2}/(\sqrt{\kappa}+1)^{2}
for any
\kappa>0
. Therefore,

P_{n}(h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}^{2})\geq(\sqrt{\kappa}+1)^{-2}P_{n}\left(\mathbb{I}\{l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}\leq\kappa\}(l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}-1)^{2}\right).

(99)

In view of (
95
), (
99
) is rewritten as

\displaystyle P_{n}(h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}^{2})

\displaystyle\geq(\sqrt{\kappa}+1)^{-2}\left\{\boldsymbol{t}^{\top}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left[P_{n}(\boldsymbol{s}_{i}\boldsymbol{s}_{i}^{\prime})-P_{n}(\mathbb{I}\{l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}>\kappa\}\boldsymbol{s}_{i}\boldsymbol{s}_{i}^{\prime})\right]\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}+\tilde{\zeta}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}n}\right\},

(100)

where
\tilde{\zeta}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}n}
satisfies the bound similar to (
96
).
From Hölder’s inequality, we have
P_{n}(\mathbb{I}\{l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}>\kappa\}||\boldsymbol{s}_{i}||^{2})\leq[P_{n}(\mathbb{I}\{l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}>\kappa\})]^{\delta/(2+\delta)}[P_{n}(||\boldsymbol{s}_{i}||^{2+\delta})]^{2/(2+\delta)}
, where the right-hand side is no larger than
\kappa^{-\delta/(2+\delta)}O_{p}(1)
uniformly in
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon_{n}}
because (i) it follows from
\kappa\mathbb{I}\{l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}>\kappa\}\leq l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}
that
P_{n}(\mathbb{I}\{l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}>\kappa\})\leq\kappa^{-1}P_{n}(l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i})
and
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon_{n}}}|P_{n}(l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i})-1|=o_{p}(1)
from Assumptions
A.2
(d)(e), and (ii)
P_{n}(||\boldsymbol{s}_{i}||^{2+\delta})=O_{p}(1)
from Assumption
A.2
(a). Consequently,
\mathbb{P}(\sup_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon_{n}}}P_{n}(\mathbb{I}\{l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}>\kappa\}||\boldsymbol{s}_{i}||^{2})\geq\lambda_{\min}/4)\to 0
as
\kappa\to\infty
, where
\lambda_{\min}
is the smallest eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}
. Hence, we can write (
100
) as
P_{n}(h_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}^{2})\geq\frac{\tau}{2}(1+o_{p}(1))\boldsymbol{t}^{\top}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}+\frac{\tau}{2}\tilde{\zeta}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}n}
for
\tau:=2(\sqrt{\kappa}+1)^{-2}>0
by taking
\kappa
sufficiently large. Because
\sqrt{n}\nu_{n}(l_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha},i}-1)=\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}^{\top}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\nu_{n}(\boldsymbol{s}_{i})+O_{p}(\sqrt{n}||{\boldsymbol{t}}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}||||\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}-\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}^{*}||)
from Assumptions
A.2
(d) and because
\tilde{\zeta}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}n}
satisfies the bound analogous to (
96
), it follows from (
98
) that, uniformly in
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon_{n}}
,

\displaystyle-\eta

\displaystyle\leq\ell_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M},\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ell_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}^{*},\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}^{\top}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\nu_{n}(\boldsymbol{s}_{i})-\frac{\tau}{2}(1+o_{p}(1))n\boldsymbol{t}^{\top}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}+O_{p}(\sqrt{n}||{\boldsymbol{t}}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}||||\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}-\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}^{*}||)

\displaystyle\quad+O_{p}(n||\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}||^{2}||\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}-\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}^{*}||)+O_{p}(||\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}||||\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}-\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}^{*}||)+O_{p}(||\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}-\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}^{*}||^{2}).

(101)

Let
\boldsymbol{T}_{n}:=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}^{1/2}\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
. From (
101
), Assumptions
A.2
(b) and (e), and the fact
||\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}-\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}^{*}||\to 0
if
\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\to 0
, we obtain

-\eta\leq\ell_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M},\boldsymbol{\alpha})-\ell_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}^{*},\boldsymbol{\alpha})\leq||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||O_{p}(1)-\frac{\tau}{2}||T_{n}||^{2}+o_{p}(||T_{n}||^{2})+o_{p}(||T_{n}||)+o_{p}(1),

uniformly in
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon_{n}}
. By rearranging the inequality and choosing a sufficiently large constant
C
, we have
||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||C-(\tau/2)||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||^{2}+C\geq 0
with an arbitrarily high probability. Namely, for any
\delta>0
, there exist
C
,
n_{0}<\infty
such that

\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon_{n}}}||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||C-(\tau/2)||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||^{2}+C\geq 0\right)\geq 1-\delta,\ \text{ for all }\ n>n_{0}.

(102)

Rearranging the terms inside
\mathbb{P}(\cdot)
gives
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon_{n}}}(||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||-(C/\tau))^{2}\leq 2B/\tau+(C/\tau)^{2}
. Taking its square root gives
\mathbb{P}(\sup_{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{M}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon_{n}}}||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||\leq C_{1})\geq 1-\delta
for a constant
C_{1}
, and part (a) follows. Part (b) follows from part (a) and Proposition
A.2
.
∎

A.6
Lemmas

Lemma 2
.

For any
M<\infty
,
\Pr\left(-\log n+\ell(\boldsymbol{W}_{i^{*}};\bar{Y}_{i^{*}},s_{i^{*}}^{2})<M\right)=\exp(-C_{T}n^{1/T})
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where
C_{T}
for
T=2,3,..
are some positive constant that depends on
M
and
T
.

Proof of Lemma
2
.

Because
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\frac{\left(Y_{it}-\mu\right)^{2}}{s_{i^{*}}^{2}}=T-1
when
i=i^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle-\log n+\ell(\boldsymbol{W}_{i^{*}};\bar{Y}_{i^{*}},s_{i^{*}}^{2})

\displaystyle=-\log n-\frac{T}{2}\log s_{i^{*}}^{2}-\frac{T}{2}\log(2\pi)-\frac{T-1}{2}

\displaystyle=-\log\left(Bn\left(\chi_{i^{*},T-1}^{2}\right)^{T/2}\right)

(103)

with
B:=\left(\frac{2\pi\sigma^{*2}}{T-1}\right)^{T/2}\exp(\frac{T-1}{2})>0
and

\chi_{i^{*},T-1}^{2}:=\frac{(T-1)s_{i^{*}}^{2}}{\sigma^{*2}}.

Therefore, to prove the stated result, it suffices to show that for any
\epsilon=\exp(-M)/B>0
,
\Pr\left(n\left(\chi_{i^{*},T-1}^{2}\right)^{T/2}>\epsilon\right)=\Pr\left(\chi_{i^{*},T-1}^{2}>\left({\epsilon}/{n}\right)^{2/T}\right)\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Given the property of the first-order statistic, the distribution function of
\chi_{i^{*},T-1}^{2}
is given by
1-[1-F_{{T-1}}(t)]^{n}
, where
F_{{k}}(t)
is the cumulative distribution function for chi-squared variables with
k
degree of freedom. It follows that

\Pr\left(\chi_{i^{*},T-1}^{2}>\left(\frac{\epsilon}{n}\right)^{2/T}\right)=\left[1-F_{{T-1}}\left(\left(\frac{\epsilon}{n}\right)^{2/T}\right)\right]^{n}.

For any finite
T\geq 2
, write

\Big[1-F_{{T-1}}\big((\epsilon/n)^{2/T}\big)\Big]^{n}=\Big\{\Big[1-F_{{T-1}}\big((\epsilon/n)^{2/T}\big)\Big]^{\frac{1}{F_{{T-1}}((\epsilon/n)^{2/T})}}\Big\}^{nF_{{T-1}}((\epsilon/n)^{2/T})}.

(104)

Then, because
(1-F)^{\frac{1}{F}}\to\frac{1}{e}
when
F\to 0
, the stated result follows from (
104
) if we can show

\frac{F_{{T-1}}((\epsilon x)^{2/T})}{x}=O(x^{-1/T})\ \text{as}\ x\rightarrow 0

(105)

for
x=1/n
, where (
104
)-(
105
) imply that
\log\Big[1-F_{{T-1}}\big((\epsilon/n)^{2/T}\big)\Big]^{n}\leq-C_{T}n^{1/T}
for some positive constant
C_{T}
when
n
is sufficiently large.

For small
t
, the CDF
F_{k}(t)
can be expanded as
F_{k}(t)=\frac{t^{k/2}}{2^{k/2}\Gamma(k/2+1)}+o(t^{k/2})\text{ as }t\to 0
. Then, letting
k=T-1
and
t=(\epsilon x)^{2/T}=(\epsilon x)^{2/(k+1)}
, and simplifying the exponent
(x^{2/(k+1)})^{k/2}=x^{k/(k+1)}
,
we have

F_{{T-1}}((\epsilon x)^{2/T})=\frac{(\epsilon x)^{k/(k+1)}}{2^{k/2}\Gamma(k/2+1)}+o((\epsilon x)^{k/(k+1)})\text{ as }x\to 0.

Therefore, given finite
\epsilon>0
, noting that
x^{k/(k+1)-1}=x^{-1/(k+1)}
, we have

\frac{F_{{T-1}}((\epsilon x)^{2/T})}{x}=\frac{(\epsilon x)^{-1/(k+1)}}{2^{k/2}\Gamma(k/2+1)}+o(x^{-1/(k+1)})=O(x^{-1/(k+1)})\text{ as }x\to 0,

and the equation (
105
) follows with
k+1=T
.

∎

Lemma 3
.

For any
M<\infty
,
\Pr\left(-4\log n+\ell(\boldsymbol{W}_{i^{*}};\bar{Y}_{i^{*}},s_{i^{*}}^{2})<M\right)\rightarrow 1
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

Proof.

Following the proof of Lemma
2
, because
-4\log n+\ell(\boldsymbol{W}_{i^{*}};\bar{Y}_{i^{*}},s_{i^{*}}^{2})=-\log\left(Bn^{4}\left(\chi_{i^{*},T-1}^{2}\right)^{T/2}\right)
, it suffices to show that, for any
\epsilon>0
,
\Pr\left(\chi_{i^{*},T-1}^{2}>\left({\epsilon}/{n^{4}}\right)^{2/T}\right)=\Big[1-F_{{T-1}}\big((\epsilon/n^{4})^{2/T}\big)\Big]^{n}\rightarrow 1
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. For any finite
T\geq 2
, write

\Big[1-F_{{T-1}}\big((\epsilon/n^{4})^{2/T}\big)\Big]^{n}=\Big\{\Big[1-F_{{T-1}}\big((\epsilon/n^{4})^{2/T}\big)\Big]^{\frac{1}{F_{{T-1}}((\epsilon/n^{4})^{2/T})}}\Big\}^{nF_{{T-1}}((\epsilon/n^{4})^{2/T})}.

(106)

Then, because
(1-F)^{\frac{1}{F}}\to\frac{1}{e}
when
F\to 0
, the stated result follows from (
106
) if we can show
\frac{F_{{T-1}}((\epsilon x^{4})^{2/T})}{x}\to 0\text{ as }x\to 0
for
x=1/n
. By applying L’Hôpital’s rule, we have

\begin{split}\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{F_{{T-1}}((\epsilon x^{4})^{2/T})}{x}=\lim_{x\to 0}f_{T-1}((\epsilon x^{4})^{2/T})\epsilon^{2/T}(8/T)x^{8/T-1}=\lim_{x\to 0}\tilde{C}_{T,\epsilon}e^{-((\epsilon x^{4})^{2/T})/2}x^{3-\frac{4}{T}}=0,\end{split}

where
\tilde{C}_{T,\epsilon}=\frac{\epsilon^{(T-1)/T}(8/T)}{2^{(T-1)/2}\Gamma((T-1)/2)}
because
e^{-((\epsilon x^{4})^{2/T})/2}\to 1
and
x^{3-\frac{4}{T}}\to 0
as
x\to 0
for any finite
T\geq 2
.
∎

Lemma 4
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
hold. Then, under
H_{0}:M=1
, for
\alpha\in[c_{1},1-c_{1}]
, (a) for any
\delta>0
,
\lim\sup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Pr(\sup_{\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}:||\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}||\leq\kappa}|R_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)|>\delta(1+||n\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)||^{2}))\rightarrow 0
as
\kappa\rightarrow 0
, (b)
\boldsymbol{S}_{n}\overset{d}{\to}\boldsymbol{S}\sim N(0,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}})
, and (c)
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}\overset{p}{\to}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}
is finite and non-singular, and (d)
\boldsymbol{t}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\alpha},\alpha)=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
for any
\alpha\in[c_{1},1-c_{1}]
.

Proof of Lemma
4
.

The proof follows that of Proposition 2 in
Kasahara and Shimotsu, 2012
.
For a vector
\boldsymbol{x}
and a function
f(\boldsymbol{x})
, let
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}^{k}}f(\boldsymbol{x})
denote its
k
-th derivative with respect to
\boldsymbol{x}
, which can be a multidimensional array. Observe that for any finite
k
and for a neighborhood
\mathcal{N}
of
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}
, we obtain

\displaystyle E||\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k}}g(\boldsymbol{W}_{i};\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)/g(\boldsymbol{W}_{i};\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)||^{2}<\infty,

(107)

\displaystyle E||\sup_{\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\cap\mathcal{N}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k}}\log g(\boldsymbol{W}_{i};\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)||^{2}<\infty

because each element of
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k}}\log g(y|\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{z};\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)
is written as a sum of products of Hermite polynomials.
Note also that the following holds:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\eta\lambda_{j}}L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)=0,\quad\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}}L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)=O_{p}(n^{1/2}),

(108)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\eta\eta\lambda_{i}}L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)=O_{p}(n),\quad\nabla_{\eta\eta\eta}L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)=O_{p}(n),

(109)

where equation (
108
) follows from Proposition
6
(a) and (c) and (
107
) and equation (
109
) is a simple consequence of (
107
).
Furthermore, for a neighborhood
\mathcal{N}
of
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\cap\mathcal{N}}\left|n^{-1}\nabla^{(4)}L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)-E\nabla^{(4)}\log g(\boldsymbol{W}_{i};\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)\right|=o_{p}(1),

(110)

\displaystyle E\nabla^{(4)}g(\boldsymbol{W}_{i};\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)\text{ is continuous in }\psi{\in\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\cap\mathcal{N}}.

(111)

Equations (
110
) and (
111
)
follow from Lemma 2.4 of
Newey and McFadden, 1994
and the fact that
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\psi}^{k}}\log g(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)
is written as a sum of products of Hermite polynomials.

Taking a fourth-order Taylor expansion of
L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)
around
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}
and using (
107
) and (
108
), we can write
L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},{\alpha})-L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)
as the sum of the relevant terms and the remainder term as follows:

\displaystyle L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},{\alpha})-L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)=

\displaystyle\quad\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}L_{n}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*})+\frac{1}{2!}(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*})^{\top}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\top}}L_{n}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*})+\frac{1}{2!}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}}L_{n}^{*}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}

(112)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3}{3!}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q}(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*})^{\top}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}}L_{n}^{*}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}

(113)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{4!}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\sum_{k=1}^{q}\sum_{\ell=1}^{q}\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}}L_{n}^{*}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}+R_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha),

(114)

where
\nabla L_{n}^{*}
denotes the derivative of
L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)
evaluated at
(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)
. In view of (
108
) and (
109
), the remainder term is written as

\displaystyle R_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)=O_{p}(n^{1/2})\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\sum_{k=1}^{q}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}+O_{p}(n)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{q}||\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*}||^{2}\lambda_{i}+||\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*}||^{3}\right)

(115)

\displaystyle+O_{p}(n)\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\sum_{k=1}^{q}\left(||\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*}||^{4}+||\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*}||^{3}|\lambda_{i}|+||\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*}||^{2}|\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}|+||\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*}|||\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}|\right)\qquad

(116)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4!}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\sum_{k=1}^{q}\sum_{\ell=1}^{q}\{\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}}L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\dagger},\alpha)-\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}}L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)\}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}

(117)

with
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\dagger}
being between
\boldsymbol{\psi}
and
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}
. Because
||\sqrt{n}t(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)||^{2}=n||\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*}||^{2}+n\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{j=1}^{i}\alpha^{2}(1-\alpha)^{2}|\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}|^{2}
, the right-hand side of (
115
) and the terms in (
116
) are bounded by
O_{p}(1)(||\sqrt{n}t(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)||+||\sqrt{n}t(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)||^{2})(||\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*}||+||\lambda||)
. In view of (
110
) and (
111
), (
117
) is bounded by
||\sqrt{n}t(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)||^{2}[d(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\dagger})+o_{p}(1)]
with
d(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\dagger})\rightarrow 0
as
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\dagger}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}
, where a function
d(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\dagger})
corresponds to
n^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}}L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\dagger},\alpha)-\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}}L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)]
. Therefore,
R_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)=(1+||\sqrt{n}t(\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)||)^{2}[d(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\dagger})+o_{p}(1)+O_{p}(||\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*}||)]
, and part (a) follows.

Part (b) follows from Lemma
6
(c) and (d), the Lindeberg–Levy central limit theorem, and the finiteness of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}
in part (c).

For part (c), we first provide the formula of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}
. Partition
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}
as

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}n}&\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}\\
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}^{\top}&\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}\end{array}\right),\quad\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}n}:q\times q,\quad\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}:q\times q_{\lambda},\quad\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}:q_{\lambda}\times q_{\lambda},

where
q_{\lambda}=q(q+1)/2
.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}n}
is given by
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}n}=-n^{-1}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\top}}L_{n}(\psi^{*},\alpha)
. For
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}
, let
A_{ij}=n^{-1}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}}L_{n}(\psi^{*},\alpha)
and write the term in (
113
) as
(n/2)\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q}(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*})^{\top}A_{ij}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}=n\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{j=1}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*})^{\top}A_{ij}c_{ij}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}
, where
c_{ij}=1/2
if
i=j
, and
c_{ij}=1
if
i\neq j
. Then, by defining
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}=-(A_{11},\ldots,A_{qq},A_{12},\ldots,A_{q-1,q})/\alpha(1-\alpha)
, the term in (
113
) equals
-n(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}[\alpha(1-\alpha)v(\boldsymbol{\lambda})]
. For
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}
, define
B_{ijk\ell}=n^{-1}(8/4!)\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}}L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)
so that the first term in (
114
) is written as
(n/8)\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\sum_{k=1}^{q}\sum_{\ell=1}^{q}B_{ijk\ell}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}=(n/2)\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{j=1}^{i}\sum_{k=1}^{q}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}c_{ij}c_{k\ell}B_{ijk\ell}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}
. Define
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}
such that the
(ij,k\ell)
element of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}
is
-c_{ij}c_{k\ell}B_{ijk\ell}/\alpha^{2}(1-\alpha)^{2}
, where the values of
ij
run over
\{(1,1),\ldots,(q,q),(1,2),\ldots,(q-1,q)\}
. Then, the first term in (
114
) equals
-(n/2)[\alpha(1-\alpha)v(\boldsymbol{\lambda})]^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}[\alpha(1-\alpha)v(\boldsymbol{\lambda})]
. With this definition of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}
, the expansion (
112
)-(
114
) is written as (
65
) in terms of
\sqrt{n}t(\psi,\alpha)
.

We now show that
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}\rightarrow_{p}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}
.
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}n}\rightarrow_{p}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\eta}
holds trivially. For
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}
, it follows from Lemma
6
(c) and the law of large numbers that
A_{ij}\rightarrow_{p}-\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}l(\boldsymbol{W};\psi^{*},\alpha)\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}}l(\boldsymbol{W};\psi^{*},\alpha)]
, giving
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}\rightarrow_{p}E\left[\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\top}/\alpha(1-\alpha)\right]=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
. For
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}
, Lemma
6
(d) and the law of large numbers imply that
\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\sum_{k=1}^{q}\sum_{\ell=1}^{q}B_{ijk\ell}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}\rightarrow_{p}\\
