-\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\sum_{k=1}^{q}\sum_{\ell=1}^{q}E[\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}}l(\boldsymbol{W};\psi^{*},\alpha)\nabla_{\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}}l(\boldsymbol{W};\psi^{*},\alpha)]\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}
, where the factor
(8/4!)=1/3
in
B_{ijk\ell}
and the three derivatives on the right-hand side of Lemma
6
(d) cancel each other out. Therefore, we have
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}n}\rightarrow_{p}E\left[\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{\top}/\alpha^{2}(1-\alpha)^{2}\right]=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
, and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}\rightarrow_{p}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}
follows.

We complete the proof of part (c) by showing that
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}=E[\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{W})\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{W})^{\top}]
is finite and non-singular.
Note that
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{W})
can be expressed in Hermite polynomials as in (
121
). Then, the finiteness of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}
follows from Assumption
3
(a) and the definition of Hermite polynomials.

To show that
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}
is positive definite, it suffices to show that there exists no multicollinearity in
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{w})
. Suppose, to the contrary, that
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{w})
is multicollinear and that there exists a non-zero vector
\boldsymbol{a}
that solves the equation
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{w})=0
for all values of
\boldsymbol{w}
. Partition
s(\boldsymbol{w})
as
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{w})=(\boldsymbol{s}_{(\mu)}^{\top},\boldsymbol{s}_{(\beta)}^{\top})^{\top}
with
\boldsymbol{s}_{(\mu)}=(s_{\mu},s_{\sigma},s_{\lambda_{\mu\mu}},s_{\lambda_{\mu\sigma}},s_{\lambda_{\sigma\sigma}})^{\top}
and
\boldsymbol{s}_{(\beta)}=(\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\top},\boldsymbol{s}_{\lambda_{\mu\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{\top},\boldsymbol{s}_{\lambda_{\sigma\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{\top},\boldsymbol{s}_{\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\boldsymbol{\beta}}}^{\top})^{\top}
,
where
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{w})
is defined in (
40
) and (
121
).
Similarly, partition
\boldsymbol{a}
as
\boldsymbol{a}=(\boldsymbol{a}_{(\mu)}^{\top},\boldsymbol{a}_{(\beta)}^{\top})^{\top}
so that

\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{w})=\boldsymbol{a}_{(\mu)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{s}_{(\mu)}+\boldsymbol{a}_{(\beta)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{s}_{(\beta)}.

(118)

By Assumption
3
(b) and the property of Hermite polynomials, if
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{w})=\boldsymbol{0}
for all
\boldsymbol{w}
, then
\boldsymbol{a}_{(\beta)}=\boldsymbol{0}
.

Then, in view of (
118
), the stated result follows if we can show that
\boldsymbol{a}_{(\mu)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{s}_{(\mu)}=\boldsymbol{0}
for all
\boldsymbol{w}
implies
\boldsymbol{a}_{(\mu)}=0
.
Suppose that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{a}_{(\mu)}^{\top}\boldsymbol{s}_{(\mu)}

\displaystyle=a_{\mu}\sum_{t=1}^{T}H^{1*}_{t}+(a_{\sigma}+a_{\lambda_{\mu\mu}})\sum_{t=1}^{T}H^{2*}_{t}+\frac{a_{\lambda_{\mu\mu}}}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{s\neq t}H^{1*}_{t}H^{1*}_{is}

\displaystyle+a_{\lambda_{\mu\sigma}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}H^{3*}_{t}+a_{\lambda_{\mu\sigma}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{s\neq t}H^{1*}_{t}H^{2*}_{is}+3a_{\lambda_{\sigma\sigma}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}H^{4*}_{t}+\frac{a_{\lambda_{\sigma\sigma}}}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{s\neq t}H^{2*}_{t}H^{2*}_{is}=0

for all
\boldsymbol{w}
,
where
H^{j*}_{t}
for
j=1,2,3
is defined in (
120
) in Appendix
A.7
.

Because the above equation holds for all values of
\boldsymbol{w}
, with the property of the Hermite polynomials, we have
a_{\mu}=0,(a_{\sigma}+a_{\lambda_{\mu\mu}})=0,a_{\lambda_{\mu\mu}}=0,a_{\lambda_{\mu\sigma}}=0,a_{\lambda_{\sigma\sigma}}=0
. This implies that
\boldsymbol{a}_{(\mu)}=0
.
Therefore, no multicollinearity exists in
\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{w})
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}
is non-singular, proving part (c).

The proof of part (d) closely follows the proof of Theorem 1 of
Andrews, 1999
. Let
\boldsymbol{T}_{n}:=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}^{1/2}\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\alpha},\alpha)
. Then, in view of (
65
), we have

\displaystyle o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle\leq L_{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\alpha},\alpha)-L_{n}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{T}_{n}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{S}_{n}-\frac{1}{2}||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||^{2}+R_{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\alpha},\alpha)

\displaystyle=O_{p}(||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||)-\frac{1}{2}||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||^{2}+(1+||\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||)^{2}o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle=||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||O_{p}(1)-\frac{1}{2}||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||^{2}+o_{p}(||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||)+o_{p}(||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||^{2})+o_{p}(1),

where the third equality holds because
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{S}_{n}=O_{p}(1)
and
R_{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\alpha},\alpha)=o_{p}((1+||\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||)^{2})
from Proposition
4
. Rearranging this equation yields
||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||^{2}\leq 2||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||O_{p}(1)+o_{p}(1)
. Denote the
O_{p}(1)
term by
\varsigma_{n}
. Then,
(||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||-\varsigma_{n})^{2}\leq\varsigma_{n}^{2}+o_{p}(1)=O_{p}(1)
; taking its square root gives
||\boldsymbol{T}_{n}||\leq O_{p}(1)
. In conjunction with
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}_{n}\rightarrow_{p}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{I}}
, we obtain
\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{t}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\alpha},\alpha)=O_{p}(1)
, and part (d) follows.

∎

Lemma 5
.

Suppose that the assumptions in Proposition
7
hold. If
-n^{-1}\log q_{n}=o(1)
, then
n^{-1}c^{M}_{1-q_{n}}=o(1)
.

Proof.

For brevity of notation,
write
c_{n}=c^{M}_{1-q_{n}}
.
By Theorem 2.1 of
Foutz and Srivastava, 1977
,
PLR_{n}(M)\overset{d}{\rightarrow}\sum_{j=1}^{K}b_{j}\chi_{j}^{2}
for
0<b_{j}<\infty
and
K
is finite, where
\chi_{1}^{2}
, …,
\chi_{K}^{2}
are independent chi-square random variables with one degree of freedom.
Then, we have

\displaystyle q_{n}

\displaystyle=\Pr\left(\sum_{j=1}^{K}b_{j}\chi_{j}^{2}\geq c_{n}\right)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{K}\Pr\left(\chi_{j}^{2}\geq\frac{c_{n}}{b_{j}}\right)\leq\frac{K}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\exp\left(-t\frac{c_{n}}{b^{*}}\right)\quad\text{for $0<t<\frac{1}{2}$}

with
b^{*}=\arg\max\{b_{1},...,b_{K}\}
, where the last inequality follows from a Chernoff bound:
\Pr\left(\chi_{j}^{2}\geq\frac{c_{n}}{b^{*}}\right)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[\exp(t(\chi_{j}^{2}-1))]}{\exp(t(\frac{c_{n}}{b^{*}}-1))}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\exp\left(-t\frac{c_{n}}{b^{*}}\right)
for
0<t<\frac{1}{2}
. Therefore,

-\frac{\log q_{n}}{n}\geq-\frac{1}{n}\log\left(\frac{K}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\right)+\frac{1}{2b_{j}^{*}}\frac{c_{n}}{n}
,
and the stated result follows.

∎

Lemma 6
.

Suppose that
g(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)
is defined as (
62
), where
\boldsymbol{\psi}=(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\top},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\top})^{\top}
. Let
g^{*}
,
\nabla g^{*}
, and
\nabla\log g^{*}
denote
g(\boldsymbol{W};\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)
,
\nabla g(\boldsymbol{W};\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)
, and
\nabla\log g(\boldsymbol{W};\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)
evaluated at
(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{*},\alpha)
, respectively. Let
\nabla f^{*}
denote
\nabla f(\boldsymbol{W};\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})
.
The following statements hold.

(a)

For
l=0,1,\ldots,\nabla_{(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\otimes\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\otimes l})^{\top}}g^{*}=0
;

(b)

\nabla_{(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\otimes 2})^{\top}}g^{*}=\alpha(1-\alpha)\nabla_{(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\otimes 2})^{\top}}f^{*}
;

(c)

\nabla_{(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\otimes 2})^{\top}}\log g^{*}=\alpha(1-\alpha)\nabla_{(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\otimes 2})^{\top}}f^{*}/f^{*}
;

(d)

\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}}\log g^{*}]=0
,
\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}}\log g^{*}]=0
,
and
\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\eta\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}}\log g^{*}]=-\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\eta}\log g^{*}\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}}\log g^{*}]
;

(e)

\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}}\log g^{*}]=-\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}}\log g^{*}\nabla_{\lambda_{k}\lambda_{\ell}}\log g^{*}+\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{k}}\log g^{*}\nabla_{\lambda_{j}\lambda_{\ell}}\log g^{*}+\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{\ell}}\log g^{*}\nabla_{\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}}\log g^{*}]
.

Proof of Lemma
6
.

Recall that

g(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\psi},\alpha)=\alpha f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\nu}+(1-\alpha)\boldsymbol{\lambda})+(1-\alpha)f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\nu}-\alpha\boldsymbol{\lambda}).

First, we show that for
l=0
holds for (a),

\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}g^{*}=\alpha(1-\alpha)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}f^{*}-\alpha(1-\alpha)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}f^{*}=0
.
For
l>0
, by Fubini’s theorem, we have

\begin{split}\nabla_{(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\otimes\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\otimes l})^{\top}}g&=\left.\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\Big(\alpha\nabla_{(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\otimes l})^{\top}}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\nu}+(1-\alpha)\boldsymbol{\lambda})+(1-\alpha)\nabla_{(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\otimes l})^{\top}}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\nu}-\alpha\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right|_{\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\boldsymbol{0}}\Big)\\
&=\left.\Big(\alpha(1-\alpha)\nabla_{(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\otimes\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\otimes l})^{\top}}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\nu}+(1-\alpha)\boldsymbol{\lambda})-\alpha(1-\alpha)\nabla_{(\boldsymbol{\lambda}\otimes\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\otimes l})^{\top}}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\nu}-\alpha\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right|_{\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\boldsymbol{0}}\Big)\\
&=0.\end{split}

To show part
(b)
, note that

\begin{split}\nabla_{(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\otimes 2})^{\top}}g&=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\Big(\alpha(1-\alpha)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\top}}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\nu}-\alpha(1-\alpha)\boldsymbol{\lambda})-\alpha(1-\alpha)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\top}}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\nu}-\alpha\boldsymbol{\lambda})\Big)\\
&=\left.\alpha(1-\alpha)^{2}\nabla_{(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\otimes 2})^{\top}}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\nu}-(1-\alpha)\boldsymbol{\lambda})+\alpha^{2}(1-\alpha)\nabla_{(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\otimes 2})^{\top}}f(\boldsymbol{w};\boldsymbol{\nu}-\alpha\boldsymbol{\lambda})\right|_{\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\boldsymbol{0}}\\
&=\nabla_{(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\otimes 2})^{\top}}f^{*}.\end{split}

For part (c),
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\top}}\log g^{*}=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\top}}g^{*}/g^{*}-(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\log g^{*})(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\log g^{*})=\alpha(1-\alpha)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\top}}f^{*}/f^{*}
because
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\top}}g^{*}/g^{*}=\alpha(1-\alpha)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}}f^{*}/f^{*}
from part (a) and
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\log g^{*}=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}g^{*}/g^{*}=0
from part (b).

For parts (d) and (e), observe that
\int\nabla_{\lambda_{i}}\log g(\boldsymbol{w};\psi,\alpha)g(\boldsymbol{w};\psi,\alpha)dx=0
holds for any
\psi
in the interior of
\Theta_{\psi}
, and differentiating this equation w.r.t.
\lambda_{j}
gives

\int\{\nabla_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}}\log g(\boldsymbol{w};\psi,\alpha)+\nabla_{\lambda_{i}}\log g(\boldsymbol{w};\psi,\alpha)\nabla_{\lambda_{j}}\log g(\boldsymbol{w};\psi,\alpha)\}g(\boldsymbol{w};\psi,\alpha)dx=0.

(119)

Evaluating (
119
) at
\psi=\psi^{*}
in conjunction with part (a) gives the first equation in part (d). Differentiating (
119
) w.r.t.
\lambda_{k}
or
\eta
and evaluating at
\psi=\psi^{*}
gives the latter two equations in part (d). Part (e) follows from differentiating (
119
) w.r.t.
\lambda_{k}
and
\lambda_{\ell}
and evaluating at
\psi=\psi^{*}
in conjunction with parts (a) and (d).

∎

Lemma 7
.

[Lemma 2.1 of
Liu and Shao, 2003
]
Suppose
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are i.i.d. random variables with
\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathbb{E}|X_{i}|^{q+\delta}<C
for some
\delta>0
,
q>0
, and
C\in(0,\infty)
. Then,
\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|X_{i}|=o_{p}(n^{1/q})
.

Proof.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle P\left(\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|X_{i}|>\varepsilon n^{1/q}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}P(|X_{i}|>\varepsilon n^{1/q})\leq\varepsilon^{-q}\mathbb{E}(|X_{1}|^{q}\mathbb{I}{\{|X_{1}|>\varepsilon n^{1/q}\}})

by a version of the Markov inequality and the i.i.d. assumption. As
n\to\infty
, the right-hand side tends to
0
by the dominated convergence theorem.
∎

A.7
Score function for testing
H_{0}:m=1
against
H_{A}:m=2

H^{j}(\cdot)
is defined as the
j
-th order Hermite polynomial.
H^{1}(t)=t
,
H^{2}(t)=t^{2}-1
,
H^{3}(t)=t^{3}-3t
, and
H^{4}(t)=t^{4}-6t^{2}+3
.
As shown in the supplementary material of
Kasahara and Shimotsu, 2015
, the derivative of
\{\frac{1}{\sigma}\phi(\frac{t}{\sigma})\}
is

\frac{\nabla_{\mu^{m}}\nabla_{(\sigma^{2})^{\ell}}\{\frac{1}{\sigma}\phi(\frac{t}{\sigma})\}}{\{\frac{1}{\sigma}\phi(\frac{t}{\sigma})\}}=\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{\ell}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}\right)^{m+2\ell}H^{m+2\ell}\left(\frac{t}{\sigma}\right).

Let
f^{*}=f(\boldsymbol{W};\theta^{*})=\prod_{t=1}^{T}f^{*}_{t}(\boldsymbol{W};\theta^{*})
, where
f^{*}_{t}
denotes the component density for observation
t
, which may be either a normal or a mixture density. Let
\nabla f^{*}=\nabla f(\boldsymbol{W};\theta^{*})
denote its gradient.

Define the Hermite polynomials evaluated at
Y_{t}-\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}-\mu^{*}
as follows:

\begin{split}H^{j*}_{t}=\frac{1}{(\sigma^{*})^{j}}H^{j}\left(\frac{Y_{t}-\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}-\mu^{*}}{\sigma^{*}}\right).\end{split}

(120)

A.7.1
Score function for the model (
1
) with normal density (
2
)

Let

f^{*}_{t}=\frac{1}{\sigma^{*}}\phi\left(\frac{Y_{t}-\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}-\mu^{*}}{\sigma^{*}}\right)
.
Then, the first-order derivatives of the normal density function (
2
) with respect to the parameters are given by:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}f^{*}=f^{*}\sum_{t=1}^{T}H^{1*}_{t},

\displaystyle\nabla_{\sigma^{2}}f^{*}=f^{*}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\frac{1}{2}H^{2*}_{t},

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}f^{*}=f^{*}\sum_{t=1}^{T}H^{1*}_{t}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}.

The score function defined in (
40
) is then written in terms of the Hermite polynomials:

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{W})=\begin{pmatrix}s_{\mu}\\
s_{\sigma}\\
\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\sum_{t=1}^{T}H^{1*}_{t}\\
\sum_{t=1}^{T}H^{2*}_{t}\\
\sum_{t=1}^{T}H^{1*}_{t}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{W})=\begin{pmatrix}s_{\lambda_{\mu}\lambda_{\mu}}\\
s_{\lambda_{\mu}\lambda_{\sigma}}\\
s_{\lambda_{\sigma}\lambda_{\sigma}}\\
\boldsymbol{s}_{\lambda_{\mu}\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\\
\boldsymbol{s}_{\lambda_{\sigma}\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\\
\boldsymbol{s}_{\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\end{pmatrix},\end{split}

(121)

where

\begin{split}\begin{pmatrix}s_{\lambda_{\mu}\lambda_{\mu}}\\
s_{\lambda_{\mu}\lambda_{\sigma}}\\
s_{\lambda_{\sigma}\lambda_{\sigma}}\\
\boldsymbol{s}_{\lambda_{\mu}\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\\
\boldsymbol{s}_{\lambda_{\sigma}\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\\
\boldsymbol{s}_{\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\end{pmatrix}&=\begin{pmatrix}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[H^{2*}_{t}+\sum_{s\neq t}H^{1*}_{t}H^{1*}_{s}\right]\\
\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[H^{3*}_{t}+\sum_{s\neq t}H^{1*}_{t}H^{2*}_{s}\right]\\
\frac{1}{4}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[H^{4*}_{t}+\sum_{s\neq t}H^{2*}_{t}H^{2*}_{s}\right]\\
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[H^{2*}_{t}+\sum_{s\neq t}H^{1*}_{t}H^{1*}_{s}\right]\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\\
\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[H^{3*}_{t}+\sum_{s\neq t}H^{1*}_{t}H^{2*}_{s}\right]\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\\
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[H^{2*}_{t}\widetilde{\textnormal{vech}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\top})+\sum_{s\neq t}H^{1*}_{t}H^{1*}_{s}\widetilde{\textnormal{vech}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\boldsymbol{X}_{s}^{\top})\right]\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

(122)

A.7.2
Score function for the model (

For brevity, we present the score function for the model (

) with a two-component normal mixture (

f^{*}=\prod_{t=1}^{T}f^{*}_{t}=\prod_{t=1}^{T}\left(\tau^{*}f^{*}_{1t}+(1-\tau^{*})f^{*}_{2t}\right),

where

for

.
In this model, we omit the covariates

and the parameter

for simplicity. The score function with covariates can be derived similarly to the previous section.

b
-th order normalized Hermite polynomial for
k
-th component evaluated at

as follows:

\begin{split}H^{b*}_{kt}=\frac{1}{(\sigma^{*})^{b}}H^{b}\left(\frac{Y_{t}-\mu_{k}^{*}}{\sigma^{*}}\right).\end{split}

\displaystyle\gamma_{1t}:=\frac{\tau^{*}f_{1t}^{*}}{\tau^{*}f_{1t}^{*}+(1-\tau^{*})f_{2t}^{*}},\quad\gamma_{2t}:=1-\gamma_{1t}.

) is then written as:

\begin{pmatrix}s_{\lambda_{\tau}\lambda_{\tau}}\\

s_{\lambda_{\mu_{2}}\lambda_{\mu_{2}}}\\
s_{\lambda_{\mu_{1}}\lambda_{\mu_{2}}}\\
s_{\lambda_{\tau}\lambda_{\mu_{1}}}\\
s_{\lambda_{\tau}\lambda_{\mu_{2}}}\\

s_{\lambda_{\mu_{2}}\lambda_{\sigma}}\\
s_{\lambda_{\sigma}\lambda_{\sigma}}\\

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\left(\frac{\gamma_{1t}}{\tau^{*}}-\frac{\gamma_{2t}}{1-\tau^{*}}\right)H^{2*}_{1t}+\sum_{s\neq t}\left(\gamma_{1t}H^{2*}_{1t}+\gamma_{2t}H^{2*}_{2t}\right)\left(\frac{\gamma_{1s}}{\tau^{*}}-\frac{\gamma_{2s}}{1-\tau^{*}}\right)\right)\end{pmatrix}.

A.8
Score function for testing
H_{0}:m=M_{0}
against
H_{A}:m=M_{0}+1

We only present score function for the model (
1
) with normal density (
2
). The score functions for other models can be derived analogously.
Let
g^{*}=g(\boldsymbol{W};\vartheta_{M_{0}}^{*})=\sum_{j=1}^{M_{0}}\alpha_{j}^{*}f_{j}^{*}
denote the true
M_{0}
-component model as in equation (
16
), where
f_{j}^{*}=f(\boldsymbol{W};\theta_{j}^{*})=\prod_{t=1}^{T}\frac{1}{\sigma_{j}^{*}}\phi\left(\frac{Y_{t}-\mu_{j}^{*}}{\sigma_{j}^{*}}\right)
is the
j
-th component density.
In this section, we omit the covariate
\boldsymbol{X}_{it}
and the parameters
\beta_{j}^{*}
for simplicity; the derivation with covariates can be done analogously to the previous section.
Define
H^{b*}_{j,it}
as a shorthand for the
b
-th order normalized Hermite polynomial evaluated at
\frac{Y_{it}-\mu_{j}^{*}}{\sigma_{j}^{*}}
, i.e.,
H^{b*}_{j,t}=\frac{1}{(\sigma_{j}^{*})^{b}}H^{b}\left(\frac{Y_{t}-\mu_{j}^{*}}{\sigma_{j}^{*}}\right)
. Define the weight
w_{i}^{j*}
as

w_{j}^{*}=\frac{\alpha_{j}^{*}f_{j}^{*}}{\sum_{l}\alpha_{l}^{*}f_{l}^{*}},\quad j=1,\ldots,M_{0}.

The score functions are

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{W})=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{w_{1}^{*}}{\alpha_{1}^{*}}-\frac{w_{M_{0}}^{*}}{\alpha_{M_{0}}^{*}}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{w_{M_{0}-1}^{*}}{\alpha_{M_{0}-1}^{*}}-\frac{w_{M_{0}}^{*}}{\alpha_{M_{0}}^{*}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}_{\mu}(\boldsymbol{W})=\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}^{*}\sum_{t=1}^{T}H^{1*}_{1,t}\\
\vdots\\
w_{M_{0}}^{*}\sum_{t=1}^{T}H^{1*}_{M_{0},t}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}_{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{W})=\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}^{*}\sum_{t=1}^{T}H^{2*}_{1,t}\\
\vdots\\
w_{M_{0}}^{*}\sum_{t=1}^{T}H^{2*}_{M_{0},t}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\boldsymbol{W})=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{s}_{\lambda_{\mu}\lambda_{\mu}}\\
\boldsymbol{s}_{\lambda_{\mu}\lambda_{\sigma}}\\
\boldsymbol{s}_{\lambda_{\sigma}\lambda_{\sigma}}\end{pmatrix},

where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}_{\lambda_{\mu}\lambda_{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}^{*}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[H^{2*}_{1,t}+\sum_{s\neq t}H^{1*}_{1,t}H^{1*}_{1,s}\right]\\
\vdots\\
w_{M_{0}}^{*}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[H^{2*}_{M_{0},t}+\sum_{s\neq t}H^{1*}_{M_{0},t}H^{1*}_{M_{0},s}\right]\end{pmatrix},\boldsymbol{s}_{\lambda_{\mu}\lambda_{\sigma}}=\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}^{*}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[H^{3*}_{1,t}+\sum_{s\neq t}H^{1*}_{1,t}H^{2*}_{1,s}\right]\\
\vdots\\
w_{M_{0}}^{*}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[H^{3*}_{M_{0},t}+\sum_{s\neq t}H^{1*}_{M_{0},t}H^{2*}_{M_{0},s}\right]\\
\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}_{\lambda_{\sigma}\lambda_{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}^{*}\frac{1}{4}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[H^{4*}_{1,t}+\sum_{s\neq t}H^{2*}_{1,t}H^{2*}_{1,s}\right]\\
\vdots\\
w_{M_{0}}^{*}\frac{1}{4}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left[H^{4*}_{M_{0},t}+\sum_{s\neq t}H^{2*}_{M_{0},t}H^{2*}_{M_{0},s}\right]\end{pmatrix}.

A.9
EM Algorithm

A.9.1
EM Algorithm for the model (
1
) with normal mixture density (
3
)

We introduce latent variables
D_{i}\in\{1,2,\ldots,M\}
and
C_{it}\in\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
, where the top-level latent variable
D_{i}
indicates which of the
M
top-level components was chosen for observation
i
, and
C_{it}
indicates which of the
K
inner components was chosen for the
t
-th observation of unit
i
under the selected top-level component. Then, the complete-data log-likelihood is given as

\log L_{c}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\mathbb{I}(D_{i}=j)\left[\log\alpha_{j}+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{k=1}^{{\cal{K}}}\mathbb{I}(C_{it}=k)\bigl(\log\tau_{jk}+\log\phi(Z_{jk,it})-\log\sigma_{j}\bigr)\right],

where
Z_{jk,it}:=\frac{Y_{it}-\mu_{jk}-\boldsymbol{X}_{it}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}}{\sigma_{j}}
.

To obtain the Maximum Likelihood estimator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}=\arg\max_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log g(\mathbf{W}_{i};\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
, we implment the following EM algorithm by alternating between the E-step and the M-step.

In E-step, given current estimates
(\alpha_{j}^{(m)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{(m)})
, compute the posterior probabilities of the latent variables as

\displaystyle\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}

\displaystyle=P(D_{i}=j|\mathbf{W}_{i},\{\alpha_{j}^{(m)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{(m)}\})=\frac{\alpha_{j}^{(m)}f(\mathbf{W}_{i};\theta_{j}^{(m)})}{\sum_{r=1}^{M}\alpha_{r}^{(m)}f(\mathbf{W}_{i};\theta_{r}^{(m)})}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\gamma_{jk,it}^{(m)}

\displaystyle=P(C_{it}=k|\mathbf{W}_{i},D_{i}=j,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{(m)})=\frac{\tau_{jk}^{(m)}\phi(z_{jk,it}^{(m)})}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{{\cal{K}}}\tau_{j\ell}^{(m)}\phi(z_{j\ell,it}^{(m)})}

for each
j=1,\ldots,M
and
k=1,\dots,K
.

In M-step, we maximize the expected complete-data log-likelihood
E[\log L_{c}|\mathbf{W},\alpha_{j}^{(m)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{(m)}]
as follows: for
j=1,...,M
,

1.

Update
\alpha_{j}
as

\alpha_{j}^{(m+1)}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}}{n},\quad\text{with }\sum_{j=1}^{M}\alpha_{j}^{(m+1)}=1.

2.

Update
\tau_{jk}
as

\tau_{jk}^{(m+1)}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\gamma_{jk,it}^{(m)}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}T}\quad\text{for $k=1,...,{\cal{K}}$}.

3.

Update
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}
as

\mu_{jk}^{(m+1)}=\frac{\sum_{i,t}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}\gamma_{jk,it}^{(m)}(Y_{it}-\boldsymbol{X}_{it}^{\top}\beta_{j}^{(m)})}{\sum_{i,t}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}\gamma_{jk,it}^{(m)}}\quad\text{ for $k=1,...,K$.}

4.

Given
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}^{(m+1)}
, update
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}
as

\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}^{(m+1)}=\arg\min_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\sum_{k=1}^{{\cal{K}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}\gamma_{jk,it}^{(m)}(Y_{it}-\mu_{jk}^{(m+1)}-\boldsymbol{X}_{it}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j})^{2}.

5.

Update
\sigma_{j}^{2}

\sigma_{j}^{(m+1)2}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{k=1}^{{\cal{K}}}\gamma_{jk,it}^{(m)}(Y_{it}-\mu_{jk}^{(m+1)}-\boldsymbol{X}_{it}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j})^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}T}.

We iterate between the E-step and the M-step until convergence.

A.9.2
EM Algorithm for the model (
8
) with normal mixture density (
10
)

In a similar manner as above section, we denote latent variables
D_{i}\in\{1,2,\ldots,M\}
and
C_{it}\in\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
, where the top-level latent variable
D_{i}
indicates which of the
M
top-level components was chosen for observation
i
, and
C_{it}
indicates which of the
K
inner components was chosen for the
t
-th observation of unit
i
under the selected top-level component. Then, the complete-data log-likelihood is given as

\displaystyle\log L_{c}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\mathbb{I}(D_{i}=j)\Bigg[\log\alpha_{j}+\Big(

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\mathbb{I}(C_{i1}=k)\big(\log\tau_{jk}+\log\phi(Z_{jk,i1})-\log\sigma_{1,j}\big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{t=2}^{T}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\mathbb{I}(C_{it}=k)\big(\log\tau_{jk}+\log\phi(Z_{jk,it})-\log\sigma_{j}\big)\Big)\Bigg]

where
Z_{jk,it}:=\frac{Y_{it}-\rho Y_{i,t-1}-(1-\rho)\mu_{jk}-\boldsymbol{X}_{it}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}+\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t-1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}\rho_{j}}{\sigma_{j}}
and
Z_{jk,i1}=\frac{Y_{i1}-\mu_{1,jk}-\boldsymbol{X}_{i1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1,j}}{\sigma_{1,j}}
.

To obtain the Maximum Likelihood estimator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}=\arg\max_{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log g(\mathbf{W}_{i};\boldsymbol{\vartheta})
, we implment the following EM algorithm by alternating between the E-step and the M-step.

In E-step, given current estimates
(\alpha_{j}^{(m)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{(m)})
, compute the posterior probabilities of the latent variables as

\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}=P(D_{i}=j|\mathbf{W}_{i},\{\alpha_{j}^{(m)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{(m)}\})=\frac{\alpha_{j}^{(m)}f(\mathbf{W}_{i};\theta_{j}^{(m)})}{\sum_{r=1}^{M}\alpha_{r}^{(m)}f(\mathbf{W}_{i};\theta_{r}^{(m)})}

\gamma_{jk,it}^{(m)}=P(C_{it}=k|\mathbf{W}_{i},D_{i}=j,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{(m)})=\frac{\tau_{jk}^{(m)}\phi(Z_{jk,it}^{(m)})}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{{\cal{K}}}\tau_{j\ell}^{(m)}\phi(Z_{j\ell,it}^{(m)})}\qquad\text{for }T>1,

\gamma_{jki,1}^{(m)}=P(C_{i1}=k|\mathbf{W}_{i},D_{i}=j,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{(m)})=\frac{\tau_{jk}^{(m)}\phi(Z_{jki,1}^{(m)})}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{{\cal{K}}}\tau_{j\ell}^{(m)}\phi(Z_{j\ell,i1}^{(m)})}\qquad\text{for }T=1,

for each
j=1,\ldots,M
and
k=1,\dots,K
.

In M-step, we maximize the expected complete-data log-likelihood
E[\log L_{c}|\mathbf{W},\alpha_{j}^{(m)},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{(m)}]
as follows: for
j=1,...,M
,

1.

Update
\alpha_{j}
as

\alpha_{j}^{(m+1)}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}}{n},\quad\text{with }\sum_{j=1}^{M}\alpha_{j}^{(m+1)}=1.

2.

Update
\tau_{jk}
as

\tau_{jk}^{(m+1)}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\gamma_{jk,it}^{(m)}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}T}\quad\text{for $k=1,...,{\cal{K}}$}.

3.

Update
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}
as

\mu_{jk}^{(m+1)}=\frac{\sum_{i,t}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}\gamma_{jk,it}^{(m)}(Y_{it}-\rho^{(m)}Y_{i,t-1}-\boldsymbol{X}_{it}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}^{(m)}+\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t-1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}^{(m)}\rho_{j}^{(m)})}{\sum_{i,t}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}\gamma_{jk,it}^{(m)}(1-\rho_{j}^{(m)})}\quad\text{ for $k=1,...,K$.}

and

\mu_{1,jk}^{(m+1)}=\frac{\sum_{i}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}\gamma_{jk,i1}^{(m)}(Y_{i1}-\boldsymbol{X}_{i1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1,j}^{(m)})}{\sum_{i,t}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}\gamma_{jk,i1}^{(m)}}\quad\text{ for $k=1,...,K$.}

4.

Given
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}^{(m+1)}
,
\mu_{1,jk}^{(m+1)}
and
\rho_{j}^{(m)}
, update
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1,j}
as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}^{(m+1)}=\arg\min_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=2}^{T}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}\gamma_{jk,it}^{(m)}

\displaystyle\times\Big[Y_{it}-\rho_{j}^{(m)}Y_{i,t-1}-(1-\rho_{j}^{(m)})\mu_{jk}^{(m+1)}-\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{it}^{\top}-\rho_{j}^{(m)}\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t-1}^{\top}\right)\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}\Big]^{2}

and

\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1,j}^{(m+1)}=\arg\min_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1,j}}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}\gamma_{jk,i1}^{(m)}\left(Y_{i1}-\mu_{1,jk}^{(m+1)}-\boldsymbol{X}_{i1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1,j}\right)^{2}.

5.

Given
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}^{(m+1)}
and
\mu_{jk}^{(m+1)}
, update
\rho_{j}

\displaystyle\rho_{j}^{(m+1)}=\arg\min_{\rho_{j}}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=2}^{T}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}\gamma_{jk,it}^{(m)}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left[\big(Y_{it}-\mu_{jk}^{(m+1)}-\boldsymbol{X}_{it}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}^{(m+1)}\big)-\rho_{j}\big(Y_{it-1}-\mu_{jk}^{(m+1)}-\boldsymbol{X}_{it-1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}^{(m+1)}\big)\right]^{2}.

6.

Update
\sigma_{j}^{2}
and
\sigma_{1,j}^{2}
As

\displaystyle\sigma_{j}^{(m+1)2}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=2}^{T}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}\gamma_{jk,it}^{(m)}\left(Y_{it}-Y_{i,t-1}\rho_{j}^{(m+1)}-\mu_{jk}^{(m+1)}(1-\rho_{j}^{(m+1)})-\boldsymbol{X}_{it}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}^{(m+1)}+\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t-1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{j}^{(m+1)}\rho_{j}^{(m+1)}\right)^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}(T-1)}

and

\sigma_{1,j}^{(m+1)2}=\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}\gamma_{jk,i1}^{(m)}\left(Y_{i1}-\mu_{1,jk}^{(m+1)}-\boldsymbol{X}_{i1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1,j}^{(m+1)}\right)^{2}}{\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{K}}\pi_{i,j}^{(m)}}

We iterate between the E-step and the M-step until convergence.
